1986-677.3   Orthogonal, symplectic, and unitary versions                       Class A

 Kind; source. theorem; P001291
 The notes. The same algebra A arises for orthogonal and symplectic infinite groups,
 while the unitary case gives A ⊗ A.
 Statement. In the tame strong-limit enlargement described in the cited paper, the
 SO(∞) and Sp(∞) constructions give the same abstract algebra A; the corresponding
 U (∞) enlargement has two factors. This comparison concerns that representation-
 theoretic completion, not an isomorphism between the finite-rank Lie algebras or
 their ordinary enveloping algebras.
 Reconstruction. The domain of the comparison is restored.
 Fate. The assertion appears explicitly in the opening paragraph of Olshanskii
 (1987). The exact primary text confirms what might otherwise look like a
 transcription error.
 Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
 result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-677.4   The small-m cases as a test of the construction                    Class A

 Kind; source. method; P001291; I. M. Gelfand
 The notes. Gelfand asks first for m = 0 and m = 1.
 Statement. The m = 0 case isolates the stable central algebra. In the ordinary RTT
 presentation, Y (gl1 ) is commutative, so m = 1 tests the first nontrivial centralizer
 without matrix commutator complications. This is a request for explanatory base
 cases, not an unresolved classification.
 Reconstruction. The two base cases are interpreted within the published comple-
 tion.
 Fate. The central description and factorization are Olshanskii (1987, Theorems 3
 and 10); the rank-one observation follows from the displayed scalar RTT relation.
 Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
 result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.

1986-678.1 Polynomial highest weights and the highest-weight                        Class E
line
   Kind; source. definition; P001291
   The notes. A representation is labeled by m1 ≥ · · · ≥ mN ≥ 0, and a vector invariant
   under upper triangular unipotents is described as unique.
   Statement. Finite-dimensional polynomial irreducible GLN (C) representations are
   indexed by partitions with at most N parts. The upper-unipotent invariants form
   a one-dimensional highest-weight space. A nonzero vector in that space is unique
   only up to multiplication by a scalar; a literal unique vector requires a normalization.
   Reconstruction. “Unique” is replaced by “unique line.”
   Fate. This is the classical highest-weight setup used in the central-character calcu-

1986-678.4 Capelli-type central coefficients and the classical                   Class E
limit
  Kind; source. construction; P001292
  The notes. The coefficients of the quantum determinant are central, and their rela-
  tion to familiar determinant or trace expressions is discussed.
  Statement. In U (glN ), the coefficients of the shifted quantum characteristic polyno-
  mial are central and algebraically independent; their leading PBW symbols are the
  elementary invariant polynomials on matrices. Other central generators, such as
  properly interpreted power-sum invariants, are polynomially related to them. Un-
  shifted determinants of noncommuting matrix entries are not automatically central.
  Reconstruction. The associated-graded meaning of the classical determinant is sup-
  plied.
  Fate. Molev et al. (1996, §2.11 and the comments in §2.24) identifies the Capelli
  and Harish–Chandra formulas and their classical antecedents. This is established
  invariant theory, with the Yangian packaging emphasized in Cherednik's talk.

1.14     I. M. Gelfand, 1986-09-01: Matroids, Coxeter
         groups, and quadratic duality
record 680; source pages P001292-P001296.
   The actual contribution begins on P001293.        Gelfand discusses work with
Serganova on matroids and torus strata, explains the Coxeter-group extension,
and then presents quadratic algebras in connection with Beilinson and Ginzburg.
The agenda also names Cherednik, but it contains no separate Gelfand–Tsetlin
result on these pages. Several elementary examples retain useful manuscript
errors: intersection-preserving closure, two-generator finiteness, and the distinction
between the two groups of order 120.
1986-680.1     Matroid closure and the exchange axiom                               Class E

  Kind; source. definition; P001293
  The notes. The closure operation is extensive and idempotent, and an exchange
  axiom is inserted above a deleted line. The first axiom instead asserts A ∩ B = Ā ∩ B̄.
  Statement. A finite matroid closure cl : 2E → 2E is extensive, monotone, and idem-
  potent, and satisfies exchange: if b ∈ cl(A ∪ {a}) \ cl(A), then a ∈ cl(A ∪ {b}). Equality
  between the closure of an intersection and the intersection of closures is not an ax-
  iom and fails for ordinary vector matroids. The condition cl(∅) = ∅ means loopless;
  it does not exclude parallel elements.
  Reconstruction. The exchange line is preserved, while monotonicity replaces the
  incorrect intersection identity.
  Fate. The classical closure and independence formulations are reviewed in Oxley
  (n.d.). The notebook has substituted a stronger, false identity for monotonicity; its
  later linear-span example identifies the intended definition.
  Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
  result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
  tion: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-680.2     Vector matroids and closed singletons                                Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001293
  The notes. For vectors e1 , . . . , ek , closure means membership in their linear span.
  The notes also write ēi = ei .
  Statement. A finite indexed vector configuration over a field defines a matroid on
  its index set by linear independence, with cl(A) = {i : ei ∈ span(ej : j ∈ A)}. A
  zero vector is a loop; proportional nonzero vectors give parallel elements. For a
  loopless representation, every singleton is closed precisely when there are no par-
  allel pairs; together these conditions mean simplicity. The labels, rather than the
  possibly repeated vectors as an unindexed set, form the ground set.
  Reconstruction. The extra simplicity hypothesis needed by the singleton assertion
  is separated from looplessness.
  Fate. This is the standard representable-matroid construction in Oxley (n.d.), and
  the starting case of Gelfand and Serganova (1987, §2).
  Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
  result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.

1986-680.3    Grassmannian strata determined by Pluecker support                  Class A

  Kind; source. theorem; P001293-P001294
  The notes. The Grassmannian is partitioned into strata by grouping similar diagonal-
  torus orbits, and a matroid determines each stratum.
  Statement. For U ∈ Gr(k, n) over a fixed field, let B(U ) be the k-subsets whose
  Pluecker coordinates are nonzero. These are the bases of a rank-k representable
  matroid. The locally closed loci with fixed B(U ) are the matroid strata. A stratum
  generally contains many torus orbits; arbitrary abstract matroids need not yield
  nonempty strata over the chosen field.
  Reconstruction. Fixed field, nonzero Pluecker support, and the distinction between
  a stratum and an orbit are explicit.
  Fate. Gelfand and Serganova (1987, §§1–2), received 30 July 1986 and published in
  1987, develops precisely the common-refinement/torus-stratum interpretation. The
  notebook names Goresky and MacPherson as collaborators on the Grassmannian
  result; this attribution is retained without treating each stratum as one orbit.
  Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
  result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1986-680.4    Coxeter presentations and the rank-two finiteness                   Class E
question
  Kind; source. definition; P001294
  The notes. The generators satisfy s2i = 1 and (si sj )mij = 1. The two-generator example
  says the group can already be infinite although m12 is specified as an integer.
  Statement. A Coxeter matrix has mii = 1 and mij = mji ∈ {2, 3, . . .} ∪ {∞} for i 6= j;
  mij = ∞ means no product-order relation. The rank-two group is dihedral of order
  2m12 when m12 < ∞, and infinite dihedral when m12 = ∞. Thus the written finite-
  integer example cannot be infinite.
  Reconstruction. The allowed value ∞ restores the intended infinite example.
  Fate. Coxeter (1934, Introduction and §8) gives the finite dihedral presentation and
  the general reflection-group presentation. The elementary finiteness question is
  settled, not a candidate open problem.

1986-680.5 Icosahedral reflections, the binary group, and the                  Class E
600-cell
  Kind; source. remark; P001294
  The notes. The notes pass from H3 of order 120 to the double cover of the order-60
  rotation group in SU (2), then to 120 vertices in S 3 and H4 of order 1202 .
  Statement. The full icosahedral reflection group H3 has order 120. The rotational
  subgroup has order 60; its inverse image in SU (2) is the binary icosahedral group,
  also of order 120 but a different group. The resulting quaternionic vertices form
  the 600-cell, whose 600 facets are tetrahedra and whose full reflection symmetry
  group H4 has order 14400. The Coxeter generators are reflections, not rotations
  about vertices.
  Reconstruction. The two order-120 groups and the dimension of the tetrahedral
  facets are distinguished.

 Fate. The finite reflection groups and their orders occur in Coxeter (1934, §9 and the
 table on p. 618). The notebook changes from a reflection group to a binary rotation
 group without marking the change; the English word “faces” must be understood
 as three-dimensional facets here.

1986-680.6   Bruhat order and Coxeter matroids                                  Class A

 Kind; source. definition; P001294
 The notes. A reduced decomposition of a occurs inside a reduced decomposition of
 b, defining Bruhat order; a <w b means w−1 a < w−1 b. A finite subset is a flag matroid
 when it has a minimum for every such order.
 Statement. For a finite Coxeter group W , Bruhat order is characterized by a re-
 duced expression occurring as a subsequence, not necessarily a contiguous sub-
 string. A nonempty subset of W , or more generally of W /WJ for a standard parabolic
 subgroup, is a Coxeter matroid when it has a unique minimum in each translated
 Bruhat order. In the quotient case use minimal coset representatives and the in-
 duced quotient order. A merely minimal element in each finite poset would impose
 no meaningful condition.
 Reconstruction. “Minimum” is read as least element; the subword criterion and
 parabolic quotient are made explicit.
 Fate. Gelfand and Serganova (1987, §8) introduces the Weyl/Coxeter-group gener-
 alization and its translated-order characterization. The construction was submitted
 before the September 1986 talk and published in 1987.
 Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
 result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-680.7   The bases determine a finite matroid                               Class E

 Kind; source. theorem; P001295
 The notes. The vector matroid is completely determined by its maximal linearly
 independent subsets.
 Statement. For any finite matroid, the independent subsets are exactly the subsets
 of its bases, so its collection of bases determines its independence, rank, and clo-
 sure data. Conversely a nonempty family of subsets of one ground set is a matroid
 basis family precisely when it satisfies the basis-exchange axiom. No realizability
 hypothesis is needed for this reconstruction.
 Reconstruction. The extension from vector matroids to arbitrary finite matroids is
 stated as background, not attributed as a new seminar theorem.
 Fate. The equivalence of basis and independence axioms is standard and stated
 in Oxley (n.d.). The notebook explains the representable example, and the same
 assertion holds abstractly.
 Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
 result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
 the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-680.9     Quadratic algebras and their quadratic duals                       Class E
  Kind; source. definition; P001295
  The notes. The work of Beilinson and Ginzburg is introduced by A = T (V )/(W ), with
  W ⊂ V ⊗ V , and A! = T (V ∗ )/(W ⊥ ).
  Statement. For a finite-dimensional vector space V over a field k and R ⊂ V ⊗ V , a
  connected quadratic algebra is A = Tk (V )/(R), where (R) is the two-sided homoge-
  neous ideal generated in degree two. Its quadratic dual is A! = Tk (V ∗ )/(R⊥ ) under
  the ordinary tensor pairing. The equality of this algebra with an Ext algebra re-
  quires Koszulness and is not part of the definition.
  Reconstruction. The distinction between quadratic duality and the Koszul Ext iden-
  tification prevents overattribution.
  Fate. The construction predates the talk: Priddy (1970, §2, Theorem 2.5) treats the
  cohomology relations for Koszul algebras. The exact modern convention is stated in
  Beilinson et al. (1996, §2). The notes contain definitions, not enough data to identify
  a particular new representation-theoretic duality theorem.
  Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
  result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.

1986-680.11 A quadratic hypersurface and its homogenized                                Class E
Clifford dual
  Kind; source. construction; P001296
                             P
  The notes. For A = S(V )/( x2i ), the orthogonal relation space consists of symmetric
  trace-zero matrices. The resulting relations are yi yj +yj yi = 2δij h, with h = yi2 central.
  Statement. Let k have characteristic different from two and V have basis x1 , . . . , xd .
  The quadratic dual of S(V )/(x21 +· · ·+x2d ) has generators yi of degree one, off-diagonal
  anticommutation, and equal squares. Equivalently it is the graded Clifford algebra
  with a central degree-two parameter h and relations yi yj + yj yi = 2δij h. Specializing
  h = 1 gives the usual Clifford algebra; h is not set to one in the graded dual.
  Reconstruction. The ground field and degree of h are specified; the summand aij xi xk
  is read as aij xi xj .
  Fate. This is a direct example of the quadratic-dual definition in Beilinson et al.
  (1996, §2), already computed in the manuscript. No later new theorem is needed
  to settle it. The claim of centrality is part of the notebook calculation and follows
  from the equality of all squares.

1986-681.2    Boundary divisors and the orbit stratification                      Class E

 Kind; source. theorem; P001297
 The notes. The compactification is smooth, has finitely many orbits, its boundary
 has normal crossings, and there is a unique closed orbit G/P .
 Statement. For the preceding wonderful compactification of rank r, the boundary
 consists of r smooth G-stable divisors meeting with simple normal crossings. Orbit
 closures are precisely their partial intersections, including the empty intersection
 X; the total intersection is the unique closed orbit, a partial flag variety G/P . At a
 point on exactly k boundary components, suitable local coordinates give their union
 by z1 · · · zk = 0, and their intersection by z1 = · · · = zk = 0. The codimension of the
 entire boundary is one, not k.
 Reconstruction. The union/intersection distinction repairs the notebook parenthesis
 defining normal crossings.
 Fate. These are De Concini and Procesi (1983, Theorem 3.1), reproduced explicitly
 as Chirivi and Maffei (2004, Proposition 1.1). The latter primary text was read
 directly.
 Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected formulation is an established
 result; no unresolved claim is inferred from this occurrence.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-681.3    Restriction of line bundles to the closed orbit                     Class E

 Kind; source. claim; P001297
 The notes. The last numbered property writes an isomorphism between line bundles
 on M̄ and those on G/P .
 Statement. Restriction gives an injection Pic(X) ,→ Pic(G/P ); the image is a specified
 sublattice of the weight lattice. Surjectivity onto all line bundles of the closed orbit
 does not hold in general. The corrected assertion determines a line bundle on X
 uniquely by its restriction, without claiming that every line bundle on the closed
 orbit extends.
 Reconstruction. The arrow is emended from isomorphism to injection.
 Fate. The precise theorem is De Concini and Procesi (1983, Proposition 8.1), re-
 stated as Chirivi and Maffei (2004, Propositions 1.2–1.4); these propositions distin-
 guish the injective restriction from its image. Thus the notebook isomorphism is an
 overstatement.

1986-681.4 The finite Hecke algebra and its commuting                                  Class E
generators
  Kind; source. definition; P001297
  The notes. The generators satisfy the quadratic and braid relations, with commuting
  condition j 6= i, i + 1.
  Statement. For q 6= 0, the type-A Hecke algebra has generators T1 , . . . , Tn−1 , relations
  (Ti + 1)(Ti − q) = 0, the adjacent braid relation, and Ti Tj = Tj Ti for |i − j| > 1. It has

  silently changing the quadratic relation.

  the alternate convention Ti 7→ −Ti . The notebook relation requires the absolute-
  distance condition.

1986-681.6     Polynomial quotients of the affine Hecke algebra                        Class B

  Kind; source. problem; P001298, upper section; I. V. Cherednik
  The notes. Cherednik asks for the target algebra and homomorphism when x2n 7→ 1,
  or more generally when a polynomial relation in xn is imposed. The problem is
  marked unsolved.
                                              Q
  Statement. Over C, let q 6= 0 and let p(z) = ra=1 (z − Qa ) be monic with every Qa 6= 0.
  The quotient of the affine type-A Hecke algebra by the two-sided ideal generated
  by p(Xn ) is a cyclotomic Hecke algebra of type G(r, 1, n), after reversing the affine
  convention. It has dimension rn n!, with a PBW basis of bounded powers of its com-
  muting Murphy elements times Tw . Repeated roots and nonsemisimple parameters
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                 46

  do not invalidate this basis theorem. For r = 2 it is the type-B Hecke case, including
  p(z) = z 2 − 1.
  Reconstruction. The polynomial is interpreted as a universal quotient relation,
  which gives a definite later solution to the question of its target. Nonzero roots
  match the invertible affine generators.
  Fate. Ariki and Koike (1994) and the independent cyclotomic construction of Broue–
  Malle supplied this later answer. The exact presentation and Ariki–Koike basis
  theorem are read in Mathas (2004, §2, (2.2)), first circulated in August 2001 and
  published in 2004. This identifies the universal quotient; it does not claim that ev-
  ery such quotient admits an evaluation back into the original n!-dimensional finite
  Hecke algebra.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the universal polynomial-quotient prob-
  lem is settled by cyclotomic Hecke algebras and their basis theorem. No generic
  semisimplicity assumption is needed for this answer.

1.16     V. Yu. Ovsienko, 1986-09-29: KdV and super-KdV as
         Euler equations
record 683; source pages P001297-P001304.
   The mathematical report occupies only the middle of P001298, between Ginzburg's
Hecke-algebra discussion and Kazakov's random surfaces. It identifies KdV and its
supersymmetric extensions with Euler equations for Virasoro-type groups; the cata-

1986-683.1     KdV as the Euler equation on the Virasoro group                   Class A

  Kind; source. theorem; P001298
  The notes. KdV is described as an Euler equation on the Virasoro group, extending
  the familiar Euler equation for a Lie group.
  Statement. For the Virasoro central extension of vector fields on the circle, the
  Euler–Arnold equation of the right-invariant L2 metric is, up to choices of signs and
  scale, ut + 3uux + cuxxx = 0. The fixed dual central coordinate produces dispersion;
  the metric and coadjoint convention are indispensable.
  Reconstruction. The metric, fixed central coordinate, and coefficient convention are
  supplied rather than read from the one-line note.
  Fate. Ovsienko and Khesin (1987, Prop. 1) proves the Euler interpretation of KdV
  and then constructs the super-extension in Proposition 2. The manuscript was re-
  ceived 8 August 1986 and published in 1987; this is the directly matching near-
  contemporary publication.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1986-683.2 Euler equations on the Neveu–Schwarz and Ramond                       Class A
superalgebras
  Kind; source. construction; P001298
  The notes. The report adds the supersymmetric KdV equations and names the
  Schwarz and Ramond algebras.
  Statement. The super Euler construction uses an inertia pairing with an inverse
  derivative on the odd component. In the Neveu–Schwarz case the antiperiodic odd
  modes have no zero mode, so this pairing is nondegenerate. In the Ramond case the
  periodic odd zero mode gives a degenerate pairing and must be treated accordingly.
  The component fields have their stated even and odd Grassmann parities; replacing
  odd fields by commuting scalar functions changes the equation.
  Reconstruction. The name Neveu–Schwarz, the inverse-derivative inertia, and the
  different zero-mode behavior are supplied from the publication. The one-line note-
  book report preserves no explicit component system.
  Fate. Ovsienko and Khesin (1987, Prop. 2 and the subsequent Ramond remark),
  received 8 August 1986 and published in 1987, gives the directly matching con-
  struction, including the degeneracy qualification.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-682.5    Uniform equilibrium density in the circular ensemble                 Class E
 Kind; source. remark; P001300
 The notes. Dyson's circular ensemble is mentioned as a case in which the limiting
 density is constant.
 Statement. For Haar-distributed unitary matrices, the normalized one-point eige-
 nangle density is constant on the circle; the corresponding large-N logarithmic-gas
 equilibrium measure is normalized Lebesgue measure. This concerns the circular
 ensemble with no external potential; the Hermitian cubic model has a nonconstant
 equilibrium density.
 Fate. Dyson (1962, Sec. IV, Thm. 6; Sec. V, Thm. 8 and Eq. (79)) defines the Haar
 unitary ensemble and its circular eigenangle density. The original paper, received
 15 September 1961 and published in 1962, was opened and these results read. The
 exact source used by the speaker is not identified.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
 hypotheses in the modern statement.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: medium

1986-682.8     Pure-gravity singular exponent and map asymptotics              Class E

  Kind; source. claim; P001302
  The notes. Z(β) ∼ (β − βc )5/2 and map coefficients have an n−7/2 factor.
  Statement. The nonanalytic part of the unrooted connected planar free energy has
  exponent 5/2. Transfer to coefficients gives a k −7/2 power multiplying exponential
  growth, with vertex parity and coupling conventions retained. Analytic background
  terms are present; rooting a map changes the power by a factor proportional to its
  number of edges.
  Reconstruction. The words nonanalytic part, weighted unrooted count, and parity
  qualification are editorially necessary.
  Fate. The cubic solution in Brézin et al. (1978, Sec. 4) supplies this singularity.
  Record 695 clarifies why unlabelled integer counts are not the coefficients of the
  raw free energy.

1.18     V. L. Ginzburg, 1986-09-29: Generalized Lie
         algebras: manuscript attribution to D. Gurevich
record 684; source pages P001302-P001305.
   The catalogue identifies Ginzburg on 29 September. The original heading on
P001303 instead reads D. Gurevich, 13 October 1986, followed by generalized Lie
algebras and groups. We retain the catalogue record but attribute the surviving
mathematical items explicitly to D. I. Gurevich. They concern involutive Yang–
Baxter operators, braided Lie identities, super and color examples, and braided
commutators. Kazakov begins later on P001304.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                  51

1986-684.1    An involutive Yang–Baxter symmetry                                 Class E

  Kind; source. definition; P001303; D. I. Gurevich
  The notes. A linear map S : L ⊗ L → L ⊗ L satisfies S 2 = 1 and S1 S2 S1 = S2 S1 S2 .
  Statement. Let L be finite-dimensional over a characteristic-zero field. An involu-
  tion S satisfying the braid equation determines representations of every symmetric
  group on L⊗n , by acting in adjacent tensor factors; dropping involutivity gives braid-
  group representations.
  Fate. Gurevich (1986, pp. 797–799) introduced this generalized formal Lie-
  theoretic framework before the manuscript talk: the note was received 10 April
  1985 and published in 1986.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-684.2 Braided antisymmetry, Jacobi identity, and bracket                  Class E
covariance
  Kind; source. definition; P001303; D. I. Gurevich
  The notes. Three identities for the bracket are given in right-acting operator nota-
  tion: antisymmetry, a three-term Jacobi identity, and a compatibility relation with
  S.
  Statement. In a symmetric monoidal category with symmetry S, a Lie object has
  a morphism b : L ⊗ L → L satisfying bS = −b and the categorical Jacobi identity.
  On ordinary vector spaces the further compatibility that b is a morphism for the
  specified tensor symmetry is essential; a bare pair consisting of any Yang–Baxter
  operator and any antisymmetric bracket is insufficient.
  Reconstruction. The categorical formulation avoids silently reversing the note-
  book's right-acting products; the bracket covariance is retained as a separate
  hypothesis.
  Fate. Gurevich (1986, pp. 798–800) states the corresponding generalized Lie iden-
  tities. The notebook is a compressed exposition of that theory, not an independent
  new axiom system.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-684.3    Ordinary and super Lie algebras as examples                        Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001303-P001304; D. I. Gurevich
  The notes. The flip gives ordinary Lie algebras; a signed flip determined by degrees
  gives superalgebras.
  Statement. For the ordinary flip S(x ⊗ y) = y ⊗ x the axioms recover Lie algebras.
  For a Z/2-graded space, S(x ⊗ y) = (−1)|x||y| y ⊗ x, with a parity-preserving bracket,
  gives super antisymmetry and super Jacobi. The degree of a nonzero homogeneous
  bracket is |x| + |y| modulo two.
  Reconstruction. Additivity of parity is a condition on homogeneous brackets, not a
  definition of the bracket of arbitrary nonhomogeneous vectors.
  Fate. These are the foundational examples recalled in Gurevich (1986, pp. 798–
  799); Lie superalgebras were already established well before 1986.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                         52

  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-684.4     Color symmetries and color brackets                                      Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001304; D. I. Gurevich
  The notes. S(ti ⊗ tj ) = a(i, j)tj ⊗ ti , with conditions on a said to follow from the axioms;
  the notes cite Trostel, Journal of Mathematical Physics 25(11), 1984.
  Statement. For an abelian grading group G, a bicharacter ε : G × G → k × satisfying
  ε(g, h)ε(h, g) = 1 defines such an involutive symmetry. A homogeneous bracket of de-
  gree zero then satisfies ε-skew symmetry and the corresponding color Jacobi iden-
  tity. Arbitrary coefficients a(i, j) need compatibility with the degrees and nonzero
  structure constants.
  Reconstruction. The grading group and bicharacter supply one standard precise
  realization of the displayed coefficients; the notes do not specify them.
  Fate. The notebook citation is identifiable as Trostel (1984), pp. 3183–3189. The
  publisher metadata matches the year, volume, issue, and subject; it does not estab-
  lish the uncertain attribution to Mosolov elsewhere in the line.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: publisher metadata checked; development: none; confidence:
  medium

1986-684.5     The braided commutator of an associative algebra                         Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001304; D. I. Gurevich
  The notes. An associative algebra produces a generalized Lie bracket by subtracting
  the product with reversed factors through S.
  Statement. If (A, µ) is an associative algebra object in the symmetric tensor category
  defined by an involutive braiding S, then b = µ − µS is a Lie object. Associativity
  alone, combined with an unrelated solution of the braid equation, does not suffice:
  multiplication must be compatible with the braiding.
  Reconstruction. The compatibility hypothesis is explicit in the modern statement;
  the brief notebook sentence leaves it implicit.
  Fate. Gurevich (1986, pp. 799–800) treats associative and Lie objects in the same
  compatible setting.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1.19     V. A. Kazakov, 1986-09-29: Ising spins on random
         triangulations and Wick expansion
record 685; source pages P001303-P001308.
   The catalogue date is preserved, but this exposition begins after the Gurevich head-
ing dated 13 October and occupies P001304–P001306. It introduces annealed Ising
gravity, a two-matrix representation, a critical temperature, and a Wick-theorem in-
tervention by A. N. Varchenko. Ginzburg's braid discussion starts with the next dated
heading and is not part of this record.
1986-685.1     Annealed Ising model on cubic planar maps                        Class A

  Kind; source. construction; P001304-P001305
  The notes. Spins σi = ±1 are summed on the vertices of a random dual graph, with
  edge interaction and magnetic field.
                                                              P        P
  Statement.
     P         For each connected cubic map Γ, use ZΓ (β, H) = σ exp[β {i,j}∈E σi σj +
  H i σi ], then sum over maps with a specified area and automorphism weight. Each
  unoriented edge is counted once. This is an annealed ensemble: summing over
  geometry is part of the partition function.
  Reconstruction. Edge counting and symmetry weights remove otherwise ambigu-
  ous factors in the notebook expression.
  Fate. Kazakov (1986), published December 1986, exactly solves a closely related
  planar Ising matrix model. Boulatov and Kazakov (1987) develops the magnetic-
  field critical behavior; its preprint circulated in 1986.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-685.2     Continuum Majorana field coupled to gravity                      Class A

  Kind; source. claim; P001305
  The notes. The model is associated with a fermionic field and gravity; the notes
  emphasize that the continuum identification is difficult.
  Statement. The critical Ising matter theory is the Majorana conformal field theory
  with central charge 1/2. Its coupling to random planar maps has continuum gravi-
  tational critical exponents. A formal identification of a discrete annealed partition
  sum with a path integral over metrics and Grassmann fields is not, by itself, an
  equality of rigorously defined measures.
  Reconstruction. The central charge and the distinction between discrete exact solv-
  ability and continuum measure construction are editorial context.
  Fate. The matrix solution of Kazakov (1986) and its extension in Boulatov and Kaza-
  kov (1987) establish exact discrete planar results; their interpretation as matter
  coupled to gravity is a further continuum argument.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 the Ising-gravity universality class and its
  exponents are established; the notebook supplies no precise measure-convergence
  theorem to classify separately.

1.20     V. L. Ginzburg, 1986-10-20: Polynomial monodromy
         and the Burau representation
record 686; source pages P001306-P001310.
   The actual mathematical section is P001307–P001308. It constructs braid rep-
resentations from cohomology of a punctured plane, identifies a Hecke relation, and
asks faithfulness of Burau. The same letter is used for an exponent and a multiplicative
monodromy parameter; the hyperelliptic example makes the ambiguity substantive.
1986-686.1     Discriminant complements and the braid group                           Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001307-P001308
  The notes. Roots are replaced by coefficients σ1 , . . . , σn ; the discriminant is removed,
  and the fundamental group is identified with the group of braids.
  Statement. The space of monic complex polynomials of degree n with simple roots
  is the unordered configuration space of n points in C, hence has fundamental group
  Bn . The ordered root space is its Sn -cover and has pure braid group as fundamental
  group. The universal punctured-plane fibration has fiber C \ {z1 , . . . , zn }.
  Fate. The topological construction underlying the Burau representation is recalled
  in Bigelow (1999, Sec. 2); it was classical before this report.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: none; confidence: high
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                  56

1986-686.2     Rank-one twisted cohomology and reduced Burau                     Class E
  Kind; source. construction; P001308
                                                              Q
  The notes. The multivalued function f α , where f (z) = (z − zi ), defines coefficients
  for first cohomology; moving the roots gives a braid representation.
  Statement. Let t = e2πiα 6= 1 be the common local monodromy about each puncture.
  Then H 1 (C \ {zi }, Lt ) has dimension n − 1. Monodromy in the configuration space
  gives the reduced Burau representation, up to duality and basis conventions. The
  universal form is over Z[t, t−1 ], and specializing t is a separate operation.
  Reconstruction. The nontrivial local-monodromy hypothesis and separation of for-
  mal parameter from exponent are supplied.
  Fate. Bigelow (1999, Sec. 2) uses the equivalent infinite-cyclic-cover construction.
  It verifies the representation being discussed, but not the notebook's attribution of
  every step to Givental.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the

1986-686.3 The Hecke quadratic relation and parameter                             Class E
conversion
  Kind; source. claim; P001308
  The notes. The braid generator is said to satisfy (Ti + 1)(Ti − α) = 0, although α has
  just denoted the exponent of f .
  Statement. For the conventional Burau generator Bi , (Bi − 1)(Bi + t) = 0. With Ti =
  −Bi , this becomes (Ti + 1)(Ti − t) = 0. Thus the Hecke parameter is the multiplicative
  monodromy t, not the additive exponent α; inverse conventions can replace t by t−1 .
  Reconstruction. The displayed polynomial is reconciled by an explicit normalization,
  not silently copied.
  Fate. The universal representation and its matrices are stated in Bharathram et al.
  (2026, Sec. 1); the notebook's formula is consistent only after a parameter renam-
  ing and sign convention.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
  tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-686.4     Faithfulness of the Burau representation                          Class B
  Kind; source. problem; P001308
  The notes. The stated goal is to prove faithfulness; the case n = 3 is already known.
  Statement. For which n is the universal reduced Burau representation
  Bn → GLn−1 (Z[t, t−1 ]) injective?     This concerns the indeterminate-parameter
  representation and does not assert faithfulness at every complex specialization.
  Reconstruction. The original all-rank question is separated from the exceptional
  rank-four case and from specialized representations.
  Fate. Bigelow (1999, Theorem 1.1), first posted 20 April 1999 and published in 1999,
  proves nonfaithfulness for n ≥ 5. Bharathram et al. (2026, Main Theorem, Sec. 1),
  posted 6 July and revised 14 September 2026, claims faithfulness for n = 4. Its
  actual theorem and PDF were read; the proof has not been independently certified
  in this project.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, faithfulness for n ≤ 3 and nonfaithfulness
  for n ≥ 5 are established; n = 4 has a recent affirmative preprint claim, which must
  be identified as such. The unrestricted conjecture in the notes is false.

1986-686.5     Hyperelliptic monodromy specialization                               Class E

  Kind; source. remark; P001308
  The notes. The final line says α = −1 yields monodromy of elliptic or hyperelliptic
  integrals.
  Statement. The double-cover interpretation corresponds to local monodromy t = −1,
  hence exponent α ∈ 21 +Z if f α is used. Its periods lie in the appropriate anti-invariant
  part of homology of the branched double cover; compactification and parity of the
  number of branch points affect the dimension.
  Reconstruction. The distinction between exponent −1, which has trivial monodromy,
  and multiplicative parameter −1 resolves the apparent contradiction.
  Fate. The Burau-cover description in Bigelow (1999, Sec. 2) supports the special-
  ization, but the notebook conflates the exponent and its exponential.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1.21     A. A. Beilinson, 1986-10-20: The Deligne–Illusie

record 687; source pages P001308-P001310.
   The short report at the top of P001309 announces an algebraic proof of degen-
eration of the Hodge–de Rham spectral sequence, referring to an Orsay preprint
by Deligne and Illusie. The theorem over characteristic zero and the new positive-
characteristic method are separate items.
1986-687.1     Hodge–de Rham degeneration in characteristic zero                    Class E

  Kind; source. theorem; P001309
  The notes. For a smooth projective complex variety, the spectral sequence from
  coherent differential forms to de Rham cohomology degenerates.
  Statement. For a smooth proper variety X over a characteristic-zero field, E1p,q =
  H q (X, ΩpX ) ⇒ HdR
                   p+q
                       (X) degenerates at E1 . Over C, algebraic de Rham cohomology
  identifies with singular cohomology with complex coefficients; projective smooth-
  ness also supplies the classical Hodge decomposition.
  Reconstruction. The indexing is standardized and smooth proper is distinguished
  from the more restrictive projective hypothesis in the note.
  Fate. The statement predates the seminar. Deligne and Illusie (1987, Introduction
  and Cor. 2.4) gives a new algebraic proof through reduction to positive character-
  istic, published in 1987.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

  Kind; source. method; P001309

  preprint at Orsay.
  Statement. If X is smooth over a perfect field of characteristic p > 0 and lifts
  smoothly to W2 (k), then                                               •
                         L the itruncated Frobenius pushforward τ<p F∗ ΩX splits in the
  derived category as     i<p ΩX (1) [−i]. For proper X with dim X < p, this yields full

                P                                            i ∂i [∆(λ) ∂i ]. Conjugation by

1986-688.5    A unitary integral with two coupled external-source                 Class B
pairs
 Kind; source. question; P001311
                                            R         ∗             ∗
 The notes. The question is to evaluate etr(A1 U B1 U )+tr(A2 U B2 U ) dU , related in the
 notes to a chiral field.
 Statement. For fixed finite matrices, the exponential has an absolutely convergent
 Taylor expansion on the compact group. Each coefficient is a finite sum of unitary
 moments computable by Weingarten calculus. In general the two-source exponent
 does not reduce to one HCIZ pair; an all-coupling planar asymptotic formula is a
 stronger question than finite-dimensional evaluability.
 Reconstruction. The convergent finite-N method is an editorial application of the
 integration theorem; it is not asserted to be the speaker's intended closed formula.
 Fate. Collins and Śniady (2006, Sec. 2, Cor. 2.4), first circulated February 2004
 and published 2006, gives all polynomial Haar moments, hence an exact coefficien-
 twise method. The notebook does not specify the source scaling needed to identify
 a unique large-N chiral-field problem. This is the same integral family as 1986-
 695.11 and 1986-698.1. [proved here] The chapter gives an exact N = 2 formula, a
 convergent finite-N moment expansion with a remainder bound, and a three-source
 obstruction to treating relative angles as independent.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026 finite-N evaluation by a convergent mo-
 ment series is available. The vague chiral-field identification is not treated as a
 separate solved or open theorem.

1986-688.6    Eguchi–Kawai reduction and its symmetry hypothesis                  Class E

 Kind; source. construction; P001311
 The notes. The integral over d unitary matrices with plaquette terms is called the
 lattice Yang–Mills partition function, containing all d-dimensional physics.
                                          P
 Statement. The one-site action is N β µ<ν Re tr(Uµ Uν Uµ∗ Uν∗ ), up to normalization.
 Equality of planar Wilson-loop equations with an extended lattice requires large-N
 factorization, unbroken center symmetry, and translation invariance in the unre-
 duced model. The untwisted bosonic one-site model breaks center symmetry at
 weak coupling in four dimensions, so the literal universal equivalence fails.
 Reconstruction. The factor of N , real part, and symmetry conditions are supplied.
 Oppositely oriented terms in the notebook can themselves make the action real.
 Fate. Eguchi and Kawai (1982) proposes the reduction. García Pérez (2020, Sec.
 2) states the exact symmetry conditions and explains the original failure; twisted,
 deformed, and adjoint-fermion variants are distinct models.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
 hypotheses in the modern statement.
 Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
 cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-688.7 Analytic solution of reduced planar gauge integrals                  Class C
in dimensions three and four
  Kind; source. problem; P001311
  The notes. The integral is underlined as unevaluated, and gauge theory as unsolved.
  Statement. Determine the large-N free energy and Wilson-loop observables of a
  precisely normalized d-matrix reduced gauge model for d = 3, 4, through its phases,
  and establish the regime in which these observables reproduce the extended-lattice
  theory. Solving a finite compact integral by Taylor expansion is not this nonpertur-
  bative planar problem.
  Reconstruction. The free-energy scaling, observables, and distinction between the
  unrepaired original model and symmetry-preserving variants make the question
  precise.
  Fate. The reduction of Eguchi and Kawai (1982) is a reformulation, not a general
  analytic solution. García Pérez (2020, Secs. 1–2) describes continuing numerical
  work; Neuberger (2020) distinguishes competing quenching prescriptions. Neither
  proves an all-coupling solution of the notebook integral.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 exact two-dimensional results and con-
  trolled reductions/numerics do not provide the requested general three- or four-
  dimensional planar solution. The original untwisted weak-coupling equivalence is
  false, and must be repaired before a continuum-physics claim.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem
  GP-1986-31
  Worth developing. Worth developing only after fixing a model and phase; a bounded
  strong-coupling coefficient calculation or symmetry test is feasible, whereas an all-
  coupling solution is not a bounded project.

1986-689.1     Heat-kernel proof of the unitary orbital formula                 Class E
  Kind; source. method; P001312
  The notes. The report starts from gt = ∆g on Hermitian matrices and states the
  determinant formula for the orbital integral.
  Statement. Compare the full Euclidean heat kernel on HermN with its radial ker-
  nel after multiplication by ∆(λ). Antisymmetrization over eigenvalue permutations
  yields the determinant of scalar heat kernels and therefore HCIZ, normalized by
  the value at a scalar matrix.
  Fate. This is the method already given in Itzykson and Zuber (1980, Sec. 3); it
  repeats 1986-688.3 with a new exposition.

  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-689.2     Spectrum of a product of positive Hermitian matrices                Class B

  Kind; source. question; P001312
  The notes. Given the spectra of H1 , H2 > 0, the notes ask how the spectrum of H1 H2
  is distributed.
                                           1/2    1/2
  Statement. The product is similar to H1 H2 H1 > 0. Two different questions must
  be distinguished: which eigenvalue lists occur, and the pushforward distribution
  when relative eigenvectors have Haar law. The first is a multiplicative spectral-
  support problem; the second needs a specified measure and cannot be answered
  by support inequalities alone.
  Reconstruction. Haar-relative-eigenbasis sampling is supplied as the natural pre-
  cise probabilistic interpretation; the note does not explicitly choose it.
  Fate. Klyachko (2000) relates product singular-value support to additive Hermitian
  spectral inequalities. Principal-minor spherical transforms, as in Faraut (2014, Sec.
  2), are relevant to the distribution question. Neither reference is claimed to be the
  exact unstated density formula intended in the note.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 finite-dimensional spectral support has
  established solutions; the notebook's unspecified distribution question is not iden-
  tified with one universal density theorem.

1986-689.3     Ellipsoid sections and interlacing arrays                           Class E

  Kind; source. theorem; P001312
  The notes. A plane section of an ellipsoid with axes a, b, c is described by a ≤ a1 ≤ b ≤
  b1 ≤ c; repeated sections form a triangular array.
  Statement. For a Hermitian operator restricted orthogonally to a codimension-one
  subspace, sorted eigenvalues interlace. Repeated principal compressions produce
  Gelfand–Tsetlin patterns. For ellipsoids, the quadratic-form eigenvalues are inverse
  squared semiaxes; the inequality must be translated consistently if the symbols
  denote geometric lengths.
  Reconstruction. Compression, axis convention, and ordering are supplied.
  Fate. Faraut (2014, Thm. 1.1 and Prop. 2.3) states the interlacing theorem and
  orbital-measure pushforward. The geometric interlacing is classical; the modern
  probability formulation is context, not a 1986 novelty attribution.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-689.5 The numerical equatorial-slab assertion in                             Class E
dimension 1000
  Kind; source. claim; P001312
  The notes. The question asks what fraction of a 1000-dimensional ball lies in a layer
  of thickness R/100 about an equator; the answer written is almost all.
  Statement. For uniform volume in the radius-R ball of dimension d, Pr(|x1 | ≤ aR) =
  Ia2 (1/2, (d + 1)/2). At d = 1000, even the generous half-width interpretation a = 0.01

  0.126. Thus the literal numerical example does not illustrate almost-full concentra-
  tion.
  Reconstruction. Two possible meanings of thickness are tested rather than choosing
  one silently.
  Fate. The formula follows from the cross-sectional volume of a ball. [computed here]
  The numerical values are independently checked by quadrature and the regularized
  beta function in the accompanying code; this is an editorial correction, not a new
  concentration theorem.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 the general concentration heuristic is stan-
  dard, but this specific dimension-to-width ratio is quantitatively false.

1986-691.1 Exact stationary phase for a Hamiltonian circle                              Class E
action
  Kind; source. theorem; P001313
  The notes. For a closed symplectic manifold and periodic Hamiltonian flow, the first
  stationary-phase term is said to give the exact integral. The written prefactor is
  (2π)−n , without a signature phase.
  Statement. Let (M 2n , ω) be compact without boundary and let H generate a circle
  action with isolated fixed points. With Liouville measure ω n /n!, the oscillatory in-
  tegral is exactly its stationary-phase sum. In local Darboux      p     coordinates, for real
  t > 0, each point contributes (2π/t)n eitH(p) eiπ sgn(Hessp H)/4 / | det Hessp H|. Consistent
  orientation and moment-map conventions fix signs.
  Reconstruction. Isolated nondegenerate fixed points, the Liouville factorial, abso-
  lute determinant, signature phase, and t-scaling are supplied.
  Fate. Duistermaat and Heckman (1982) proves exactness; Duistermaat and Heck-
  man (1983, Thm. (1.5)) treats nonisolated fixed sets. The notebook calls its own
  formula approximate, appropriately, but its displayed constant and phases cannot
  be retained literally.

1986-691.2 Coadjoint-orbit symplectic form and the unitary                           Class E
integral
  Kind; source. construction; P001313
  The notes. For X = U ∗ µU , the form is written ω(X)(ξ, η) = tr(X[ξ, η]), with ξ, η called

     ∗       ∗                            ∗      ∗
  ad ξX, ad ηX, the orbit form is ωX (ad ξX, ad ηX) = hX, [ξ, η]i, up to a uniform sign.
  It descends modulo the stabilizer; generators are not literally arbitrary tangent
  matrices. A regular unitary orbit is U (N )/U (1)N ; repeated eigenvalues enlarge the

  repair the notation.

1986-691.3 Higher stationary-phase terms and characteristic                  Class E
numbers
  Kind; source. question; P001313; I. M. Gelfand
  The notes. Gelfand asks when the second term vanishes, when the first term is
  exact, and how characteristic numbers can arise.
  Statement. Within compact Hamiltonian torus geometry, localization on a fixed
  component integrates the exponential of the restricted symplectic form divided by
  the equivariant Euler class of its normal bundle. Curvature expansions produce
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                  66

  characteristic classes and a finite-degree contribution on each component. Outside
  a specified Hamiltonian phase-amplitude class, vanishing of one coefficient does
  not characterize exactness.

  bounded scope of the informal question.
  Fate. Duistermaat and Heckman (1983, Thm. (1.5) and its expansion on pp. 154–
  155) already gives the fixed-component and characteristic-class mechanism. It is
  a precise partial answer to the intervention, not a classification of all oscillatory
  integrals.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 the localization answer in the specified
  subclass is established. The note does not define a general classification problem
  beyond it.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1986-692.2    Contraction from spherical functions to HCIZ                                   Class E

  Kind; source. method; P001314-P001315
  The notes. The notes say formula (2), the exponential orbital integral, follows from
  the first formula.
  Statement. With fixed Hermitian X, let Rτ = I + X/τ > 0 for sufficiently large τ ,

1986-692.3 Characters, highest weights, and spherical matrix                 Class E
coefficients
  Kind; source. construction; P001315
  The notes. For integral parameters the formula is related to Weyl's character for-
  mula; arbitrary complex parameters are interpreted through representations of
  GLN (C).
  Statement. Dominant integral highest weights give Schur characters, expressed by
  the alternant divided by a Vandermonde. Averaging a highest-weight coefficient
  gives a normalized spherical function on GLN (C)/U (N ). Complex continuation de-
  fines the spherical family, but arbitrary complex inducing parameters do not give
  unitary representations.

1.27     V. G. Drinfeld, 1986-11-03: Quantizing KdV and the
         second Gelfand–Dickey bracket
record 693; source pages P001314-P001320.
   The section actually runs from the lower part of P001315 through P001318. It
distinguishes the compatible KdV brackets, their free-field and Miura realizations,
higher-order operators, and quantum commuting integrals. Gelfand and Feigin inter-
vene. Three final questions concern quantization of KdV integrals, their spectrum in

1986-693.1     The two compatible Poisson structures of KdV                        Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001315
  The notes. The notes write P1 = −2∂ and P2 = − 21 ∂ 3 − u∂ − ∂u, then ask about quan-
  tization.
  Statement. On local differential-polynomial functionals of a smooth periodic field
  u, or with boundary conditions eliminating integration-by-parts terms, P1 , P2 define
  compatible Hamiltonian operators. Every constant linear combination is Poisson.
  Appropriate Hamiltonians and rescaling of u, x, t give the KdV hierarchy; coefficients
  cannot be compared across conventions without the rescaling.
  Reconstruction. The class of functionals and boundary conditions are supplied; the
  notebook does not fix a functional-analytic phase space.
  Fate. Drinfeld and Sokolov (1985, Secs. 2–3) surveys the classical structures with
  proofs, following the Gelfand–Dickey theory. The Russian original appeared in 1984,
  the English translation in 1985.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-693.4 The second bracket as the Virasoro Lie–Poisson                          Class E
structure
  Kind; source. construction; P001316
  The notes. Fourier expansion of the second bracket yields the Virasoro algebra and
  Kirillov's orbit picture.
  Statement. The affine hyperplane of fixed central coordinate in the dual Virasoro
  algebra has a Lie–Poisson bracket of the form a∂ 3 + 2u∂ + u′ , up to sign and scale.
  Quantization replaces the Fourier-mode Poisson brackets by Virasoro commutators.
  This identifies the algebra of fields; it does not by itself order the nonlinear Hamil-
  tonians.

  Poisson identification; Bazhanov et al. (1996, Eq. (2), (11)–(14)) separates it from
  the quantum charges.

1986-693.5     Classical local commuting KdV Hamiltonians                         Class E

  Kind; source. theorem; P001316
  The notes. The integrals Fn are said to commute, and a quantum analogue is at-
  tributed to Zamolodchikov.
  Statement. For a scalar second-order Lax operator, residues of appropriate odd
  half-integer powers produce local functionals forming a Poisson-commutative hier-
  archy. They are defined modulo total derivatives; periodicity or decay makes their
  integrals well defined. Classical commutativity does not imply commutativity after
  an arbitrary ordering of modes.
  Reconstruction. The formal pseudodifferential convention and total-derivative quo-
  tient are supplied.
  Fate. Drinfeld and Sokolov (1985, Sec. 2) gives the classical residue construction.
  The quantum question is separated as GP-1986-32.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

  Kind; source. problem; P001316; P001318

  The notes. L = ∂ n + un−2 ∂ n−2 + · · · + u0 = (∂ + v1 ) · · · (∂ + vn ), with   vi = 0, is associated

1986-693.12 The sweep method as a Riccati or Miura                                 Class E
transformation
  Kind; source. method; P001317-P001318; I. M. Gelfand
  The notes. Gelfand considers y ′′ − a(x)y = 0, propagates y ′ + αy = 0, and writes
  α′ − α2 = 0 with an exclamation mark.
  Statement. On an interval where a chosen solution does not vanish, α = −y ′ /y
  satisfies α′ − α2 = −a. Conversely this Riccati equation yields the factorization
  ∂ 2 − a = (∂ − α)(∂ + α). Zeros of y create Riccati poles; propagation must then change
  charts or use a matrix first-order system.
  Reconstruction. The omitted forcing −a, zero-free interval, and chart limitation are
  supplied.
  Fate. The factorization mechanism is the scalar Miura construction discussed
  in Drinfeld and Sokolov (1985, Sec. 3.7). [proved here] The corrected sign and
  nonzero right-hand side follow directly by differentiating −y ′ /y; the scan confirms
  that zero is the actual notebook reading.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-693.13     Higher-order and elliptic boundary propagation                    Class F

  Kind; source. remark; P001318; I. M. Gelfand
  The notes. Gelfand says the sweep method also works in higher order and sketches

  Statement. Higher-order scalar ODEs can be converted to first-order systems and

  contour propagation leads to boundary operator families and is not the same finite-
  dimensional Riccati equation. The note gives no domain, regularity, solvability, or
  operator formula for the PDE version.

  tified PDE claim.

  Drinfeld and Sokolov (1985); no exact Keldysh theorem is identified with the elliptic
  sketch.

  lished; this specific elliptic-boundary assertion lacks sufficient data for assessment.
  Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: thematic only;

1986-693.15 Spectrum of quantum KdV charges on irreducible                     Class C
Virasoro modules
  Kind; source. problem; P001318
  The notes. Question 1(b) asks to calculate the spectrum in irreducible representa-
  tions of the Virasoro algebra, immediately after quantizing the KdV integrals.
  Statement. Fix c, h ∈ C, the irreducible highest-weight module L(c, h), and normal-
  ized commuting local KdV charges. Determine their joint generalized eigenspaces
  and nilpotent actions grade by grade, and a complete all-grade parameterization
  where available. An ordinary eigenbasis requires semisimplicity, which is not au-
  tomatic outside a positive unitary setting.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                      74

  Reconstruction. The spectrum is read as the spectrum of the quantum KdV family,
  not merely L0 . The highest-weight restriction and generalized-eigenspace target

  gram. The opened v1 of Masoero and Ruzza (2026, Defs. 6.5–6.6, Prop. 6.7, Thm.
  6.8, Sec. 7) gives a 2026 preprint completeness theorem at c = −2, h = (β 2 − 1)/8,
  β∈/ Z, for admissible QQ solutions modulo entire-function gauge, and diagonalizes
  the first three charges in its free-field realization. This does not settle arbitrary c, h.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 the general formulation remains only partly
  resolved. [computed here] This chapter computes the level-two charge and proves
  a nonunitary Jordan block at c = 1, h = −1/32 survives in the irreducible quotient;
  the result is explicitly a bounded calculation, with no historical novelty claim.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
  read the result; development: developed in this chapter; confidence: high; problem
  GP-1986-33
  Worth developing. Worth a bounded development: exact low-grade matrices and

1.28     A. A. Beilinson, 1986-11-10: Virasoro
         uniformization and differential equations on moduli
         of curves
record 694; source pages .
   The catalogue gives no page range, but the original P001319 heading clearly
names Beilinson and 10 November 1986. The coherent continuation P001319–
P001321 supplies surviving notes: formal-coordinate uniformization of pointed
curves, determinant-line twists, and differential equations obtained from singu-
lar Virasoro vectors. This attribution is an explicit editorial recovery from the
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                      75

manuscript; the empty catalogue page field is retained. The note credits Kontsevich

1986-694.1     The universal pointed curve and its dimensions                        Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001319
  The notes. The diagram X → M has a genus-g curve as fiber; the displayed dimen-
  sion is 6g − 6.
  Statement. For g ≥ 2, the smooth moduli stack Mg has complex dimension 3g − 3,
  hence real dimension 6g − 6. Its universal curve is Mg,1 → Mg , with total complex
  dimension 3g −2. On a coarse moduli space a universal family need not exist without
  a level structure or stack interpretation.
  Reconstruction. The genus restriction, complex versus real dimension, and stack
  interpretation are supplied.
  Fate. This is classical moduli theory, used explicitly in Ben-Zvi and Frenkel (2004,
  Sec. 1.1 and Sec. 4.2). The notebook dimension is correct only as a real dimension
  of the base.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-694.2     Cotangent and Hodge determinant line twists                           Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001319
  The notes. For integers j, c, the fiber at (K, x) is (Ω1K,x )⊗j ⊗ [det Ω1 (K)]c .
  Statement. On Mg,1 , write ψ for the cotangent line at the marked point and λ =
  det π∗ ωπ for the Hodge line pulled back from Mg . The displayed bundle is ψ ⊗j ⊗ λ⊗c ,
  with negative integral powers interpreted as duals. A complex central parameter
  generally defines a twist of differential operators, not an ordinary fractional tensor
  power of a line bundle.
  Reconstruction. The notation Ω1 (K) is interpreted as the finite-dimensional space of
  global holomorphic differentials. The exponent c is not identified numerically with
  a Virasoro central charge without fixing the central-extension convention.
  Fate. Beilinson and Schechtman (1988), received 12 February 1988 and published
  that December, develops determinant bundles and Virasoro action. Ben-Zvi and
  Frenkel (2004, Sec. 4.2) treats both actual line bundles and arbitrary complex
  twists.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-694.3 Formal-coordinate uniformization and the Virasoro                        Class A
lift
   Kind; source. theorem; P001319-P001320
   The notes. The space X  b 3 (K, x, q) carries an action of formal vector fields; tC[[t]]∂t
   changes the coordinate and the remaining modes vary the curve.
   Statement. The formal-coordinate torsor over Mg,1 has a transitive infinitesimal
   action of Der C((t)), compatible with the coordinate-change group Aut C[[t]]. The
   central generator acts trivially on the base; the lift to determinant-line differential
   operators is governed by the Virasoro central extension. This is an infinitesimal
   algebraic statement, not a global action of an unspecified infinite-dimensional Lie
   group.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                    76

  Reconstruction. The distinction between the centerless action on coordinates and
  the central lift on a line twist makes the notebook shorthand precise.
  Fate. The directly corresponding publication is Beilinson and Schechtman (1988).
  The action and its determinant-line lift are stated and used in Ben-Zvi and Frenkel
  (2004, Sec. 1.1 and Prop. 4.2.3), first circulated January 2003. The precise inde-
  pendent Kontsevich priority is recorded from the notebook, without an identified
  separate manuscript. Esnault and Tsai (2000, Thm. 1.1), circulated October 1999
  and published in 2000, corrects the original paper's Theorem 2.3.1(ii), a divisor-
  twist reduction step; this later repair does not invalidate the uniformization princi-
  ple.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-694.4 Kodaira–Spencer tangent space through                                   Class E
punctured-curve vector fields
  Kind; source. method; P001320
  The notes. The tangent quotient is written as Vir/Φq (T (K\X)), then with the point x
  in the denominator; two horizontal lifts are compared.
  Statement. For a smooth pointed curve C, p with formal coordinate q, the tangent
  space to the coordinate torsor is the quotient Der C((q))/H 0 (C \ {p}, TC ), where alge-
  braic vector fields have finite-order poles at p and are expanded in q. Comparing
  trivializations of an infinitesimal deformation on the formal disc and punctured com-
  plement gives the quotient; changing the complement lift adds a global punctured-
  curve vector field.
  Reconstruction. The correct point removal, algebraic rather than arbitrary holo-
  morphic vector fields, and finite Laurent pole condition are supplied.
  Fate. Ben-Zvi and Frenkel (2004, Sec. 4.2) gives the uniformization and stabilizer
  formulation. The notebook itself explains the two-lift mechanism. The capital X in
  the first denominator is incompatible with the adjoining definitions.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
  tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-694.5 Singular Virasoro vectors produce canonical                             Class A
differential operators
  Kind; source. method; P001321
  The notes. A reducible Verma module M(c,g) contains a highest-weight vector v ′ =
  L(v); applying L ∈ U (Vir) to a lifted section gives an equation between line twists.
  Statement. Given a singular vector of definite grade in a Virasoro highest-weight
  module, localization on the formal-coordinate torsor, followed by equivariant de-
  scent, produces a differential operator between the appropriate twisted sheaves.
  Contragredient conventions determine the direction and weight shift. The con-
  struction requires compatibility with the coordinate-change action; a section is rep-
  resented by an equivariant function, not literally by a single vector of a fixed Verma
  module.
  Reconstruction. The highest weight written g is renamed h when needed to avoid
  confusing it with curve genus. No unrecorded central-charge-to-Hodge-power for-
  mula is imposed.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                 77

  Fate. The genus-zero null-vector mechanism occurs in Belavin et al. (1984). Its
  moduli-space globalization belongs to Beilinson and Schechtman (1988); Ben-Zvi
  and Frenkel (2004, Secs. 2.2–2.4 and 4.2) gives a precise localization framework.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-694.6 Finite-rank solution local systems require a specified                Class F
conformal-block sector
  Kind; source. claim; P001321
  The notes. The closing sentence says that solutions of the singular-vector equations
  form a finite-rank local system.
  Statement. A system of differential equations gives a finite-rank local system on a
  smooth locus when its corresponding differential module is an integrable connec-
  tion of finite rank there. Mere reducibility of one arbitrary Virasoro Verma module
  does not establish this property. To identify the intended conformal-block theo-
  rem one needs the representations, insertions, quotient relations, and finiteness
  hypotheses, none of which are recorded.
  Reconstruction. The missing finiteness condition is exposed rather than supplied
  by silently choosing a minimal model.
  Fate. The null-vector equations of Belavin et al. (1984) and localization of Beilinson
  and Schechtman (1988) explain the context. They are not evidence for an uncondi-
  tional finite-rank theorem for every reducible Verma module.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 finite-rank conformal-block systems exist
  under substantial additional hypotheses, but this particular unrestricted closing
  assertion is insufficiently specified.
  Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: thematic only;
  verification: indirect; development: none; confidence: medium

1986-695.2    Vertex area weights are not curvature defects                       Class E

  Kind; source. claim; P001322-P001323
  The notes. The notes set σi = ni /3, call it a defect, and explain it by dividing each
  regular triangle among its three vertices.
  Statement. If each equilateral triangle has unit area and ni triangles meet at vertex i,
  its barycentric area allocation is ni /3, and summing these allocations counts faces.
  The angular curvature defect is instead 2π − ni π/3 at an interior vertex. The two
  quantities encode different geometric data.
  Reconstruction. The elementary incidence and angle calculation is an editorial cor-
  rection, explicitly marked as ours.
  Fate. The discrete area and curvature observables are distinguished in Boulatov
  et al. (1986). [proved here] Counting triangle–vertex incidences gives the area
  identity and directly explains the notebook diagram; it does not turn ni /3 into an
  angle defect.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
  cation: indirect; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-695.3 Piecewise affine Dirichlet energy and the equilateral                Class E
restriction
  Kind; source. claim; P001322-P001323
  The notes. The integrands are said to agree for regular embeddings, and a fixed
  metric with a linear map on each triangle is proposed.
  Statement. For a piecewise affine map on a Euclidean triangulated surface, its
  Dirichlet energy is a sum of edge squared differences weighted by cotangents of
  the opposite angles, up to the chosen factor 1/2. Equilateral triangles make these
  weights constant. For an arbitrary intrinsic triangulation the unweighted graph
  energy is a different discretization; target images being equilateral is not the nec-
  essary intrinsic-metric condition.
  Reconstruction. The intrinsic domain metric is separated from the shape of its tar-
  get image; the edge-energy constant must be fixed before claiming equality.
  Fate. The dynamical-triangulation model of Boulatov et al. (1986) fixes regular in-
  trinsic triangles. The statement in the notes is a normalization-level discretization
  observation, not a theorem of convergence to the continuum path integral.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: indirect; development: none; confidence: high

1986-695.4 Gauge topology does not require abandoning cubic                      Class E
lattices
  Kind; source. question; P001323; I. M. Gelfand
  The notes. Gelfand asks how to write Yang–Mills when characteristic classes appear,
  saying that one cannot restrict to a cubic lattice.
  Statement. The relevant precise issue is a geometric definition of topological charge
  for lattice gauge fields with a controlled continuum limit. A hypercubic cellulation
  does not preclude such a definition: admissibility or small-curvature restrictions
  can separate topological sectors. The notebook does not specify a different pro-
  posed characteristic-class discretization.
  Reconstruction. The topological-charge formulation is the narrowest concrete inter-
  pretation supported by the intervention; it is not an invented general open gauge
  problem.
  Fate. Lüscher (1982), circulated June 1981 and published in 1982, constructs an
  integer topological charge for sufficiently small-action-density SU (2) fields on a
  four-dimensional hypercubic lattice, invariant under continuous admissible defor-
  mations and converging to the continuum Chern number. Thus the claimed ob-
  struction to cubic lattices is too strong.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
  cation: publisher metadata checked; development: none; confidence: high

1986-695.5 High-dimensional concentration does not make every                        Class F
graph triangle equilateral
  Kind; source. claim; P001323; I. S. Zakharevich
  The notes. Zakharevich says a random embedding of any triangulated sphere makes
  all its triangles regular on average, appealing to concentration near a shell.
  Statement. Independent identically distributed isotropic vertex vectors can have
  pair distances concentrated at a common scale in large target dimension. Coordi-
  nates distributed by a graph Gaussian, however, have edge variances determined
  by the graph covariance and effective resistances; those variances need not be
  equal. A shell-concentration slogan alone therefore does not justify the asserted
  equilateral replacement.
  Reconstruction. The two different random-embedding laws are distinguished edito-
  rially; no new asymptotic result is claimed.
  Fate. Boulatov et al. (1986) studies limiting target dimensions, but its verified ab-
  stract does not supply this universal equilateral theorem. No probability law, limit,
  or error estimate is stated in the notebook.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 concentration statements are available for
  specified distributions; this unrestricted heuristic is not an identified theorem.
  Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: thematic only;
  verification: indirect; development: none; confidence: medium

1986-695.6 Target-dimension factorization and removal of                              Class E
translation modes
  Kind; source. method; P001323; I. S. Zakharevich
  The notes. The n-dimensional embedding integral is written as the n-th power of a
  one-dimensional Gaussian integral; the parameter n is then continued analytically.
  Statement. Fix one vertex of a connected graph and write its scalar energy as xT L∗ x,
  with positive reduced Laplacian L∗ . The scalar integral is π (v−1)/2 (det L∗ )−1/2 , and n
  independent coordinates give its n-th power for integer n ≥ 0. The positive deter-

 minant permits a specified entire exponential in complex n for each fixed graph.
 Analyticity of an infinite map sum requires separate convergence control.
 Reconstruction. The pinned vertex and measure normalization are explicit editorial
 repairs; the unpinned integral is infinite.
 Fate. The Gaussian-embedding model is the one analyzed by Boulatov et al. (1986).
 [proved here] Diagonalizing L∗ gives the displayed finite Gaussian factor and ex-
 poses the missing translational zero-mode removal.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
