1986-695.7   The solvable dimensions zero and minus two                         Class A

 Kind; source. claim; P001323
 The notes. Kazakov says the sum can be calculated at n = 0 and n = −2.
 Statement. At n = 0 the matter determinant disappears, leaving weighted pure pla-
 nar maps. At n = −2, after fixing the Gaussian normalization, the weight contains
 det L∗ ; the matrix-tree theorem identifies this determinant with the number of span-
 ning trees of the graph. Negative target dimension is determinant analytic contin-
 uation, not a Euclidean embedding in a negative-dimensional space.
 Reconstruction. The determinant interpretation supplies the precise meaning of the
 formal negative dimension; the word only is retained as a 1986 contextual remark,
 not an eternal no-go theorem.
 Fate. Pure-map solvability is explicit in Brézin et al. (1978, Sec. 4). Boulatov et al.
 (1986) identifies D = −2 as exactly solvable and reports its logarithmic mean-
 square-size behavior. The article appeared in December 1986 after September
 preprint circulation.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
 hypotheses in the modern statement.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 partial; verification: publisher metadata checked; development: none; confidence:

 , with a fixed Gaussian normalization. A term tr M k inserts a vertex of valency

1986-695.11 Two diagonal source terms and the principal chiral                               Class B
model
  Kind; source. question; P001325
  The notes. Equation (12) has tr(p1 U −1 p1 U ) + tr(p2 U −1 p2 U ), with diagonal p1 , p2 , and is
  identified with a two-dimensional principal chiral field at large N .
  Statement. For finite N and specified source matrices the compact integral is entire
  in the couplings, and its Taylor coefficients are exactly given by unitary Weingarten
  calculus. A planar chiral-field equivalence additionally needs a prescribed source
  eigenvalue distribution, scaling, and a justified reduction or quenching procedure.
  Exact field-theory information does not automatically evaluate the unspecified finite
  integral.
  Reconstruction. The distinction between an exact compact integral and a continuum
  planar equivalence is editorial; the latter is not assigned an artificial solved status.
  Fate. Collins and Śniady (2006, Cor. 2.4) supplies a convergent finite-N coefficient
  method. This is the same two-source integral family as 1986-688.5 and GP-1986-
  60; the chapter development gives explicit bounded coefficients and a remainder
  estimate.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 exact finite-dimensional coefficient evalu-
  ation is established; the notebook does not specify a unique continuum reduction
  theorem.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1986-695.12        Three-state spins and a three-matrix potential                             Class B

  Kind; source. construction; P001325
  The notes. Equation (13) uses three Hermitian matrices, symmetric pair couplings,
  and quartic self-interactions, with Z3 spins said to replace Ising spins.
  Statement. Three matrix species with equal unlike-species covariance encode a
  three-state Potts interaction on the corresponding ribbon graphs. A quartic vertex
  generates four-valent dual graphs, so calling the same model a sum of purely tri-
  angular dual faces needs correction or a duality convention. With the printed sign,
  a positive quartic coupling destabilizes the real integral; the Feynman expansion is
  formal.
  Reconstruction. The Potts interpretation, graph valency, and formal-integral quali-
  fication prevent a false exact publication match.
  Fate. Daul (1995, Sec. 1), written November 1994 and posted February 1995, treats
  the q-state random-planar Potts matrix model and computes three-state scaling in-
  formation. Its displayed potential is cubic; it is not an exact all-coupling evaluation
  of the notebook quartic integral.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-695.14      The higher-dimensional gauge integral recurs                    Class C

  Kind; source. problem; P001325
  The notes. The one-site unitary plaquette integral is repeated; d = 2 is called solved
  and d = 3, 4 are requested.
  Statement. The problem is the nonperturbative planar free energy and Wilson-loop
  observables of a fixed d-matrix plaquette model in dimensions three and four, to-
  gether with a justified center-symmetric volume-reduction regime. The printed ac-
  tion also needs a real part, or an explicit sum over both plaquette orientations, for
  the usual real Euclidean gauge weight.

  needed to formulate the literal target accurately.
  Fate. Eguchi and Kawai (1982) is the original reduction; García Pérez (2020, Secs.
  1–2) explains the center-symmetry obstruction and modern variants. This is a re-
  peat of GP-1986-31, not a new independent problem.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 the general three- and four-dimensional
  analytic planar solution is not supplied by the established reduction methods; the
  original weak-coupling four-dimensional untwisted equivalence fails.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

  GP-1986-31
  Worth developing. Worth a bounded coefficient or symmetry calculation for a spec-
  ified model; the unrestricted all-coupling higher-dimensional planar solution is a

record 696; source pages P001324-P001329.
    The manuscript section P001326–P001327 names a joint project with B. V. Lidskii

1986-696.1 Projective geometries and continuous geometries as                   Class E
lattice antecedents
  Kind; source. remark; P001326
  The notes. Gelfand lists Dedekind, Weyl on projective axioms, and von Neumann on
  factors; dimension is described by lengths of chains, with Desargues in dimension
  at least three.
  Statement. The lattice of subspaces of an n-dimensional vector space is comple-
  mented and modular with maximal strict-chain length n. Projective dimension is
  n − 1. Incidence projective spaces of projective dimension at least three are Desar-
  guesian; arbitrary projective planes need not be. Von Neumann continuous geome-
  tries replace finite rank by a continuous dimension theory under additional lattice-
  completeness conditions.
  Reconstruction. The vector-space/projective-dimension shift and the distinction be-
  tween finite projective geometry and continuous geometry are made explicit.
  Fate. The coordinatization framework is developed and precisely recalled in Her-
  rmann (1984, Introduction and Sec. 2). The notebook compresses several classical
  theories and does not specify the separate work attributed to the uncertain name
  Disona.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-696.2    Two and three subspaces: finite indecomposable                    Class E
types
  Kind; source. theorem; P001326
  The notes. Two subspaces are called trivial; three decompose into one-dimensional
  pieces and a configuration of three lines. The associated free modular lattice has
  28 elements.
  Statement. An ordered triple of subspaces of a finite-dimensional vector space over
  a field decomposes into one-dimensional blocks, each subspace being zero or the
  entire line, and copies of the two-dimensional block of three distinct lines. The
  decomposition is unique up to isomorphism and order of blocks. Separately, the
  abstract free modular lattice on three generators has 28 elements; this counts lat-
  tice terms modulo modular identities, not indecomposable representation types.
  Reconstruction. The field, ordering, finite dimension, and difference between
  lattice-element and representation counts are supplied.
  Fate. Gel’fand and Ponomarev (1971, Opening paragraph) recalls the elementary
  two- and three-subspace cases. Herrmann (1983, p. 513) confirms the 28-element
  free modular lattice. These facts long precede the talk.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-696.3 Four-subspace classification and its parameter                    Class E
families
  Kind; source. theorem; P001326
  The notes. The four-subspace problem is attributed to long work of Gelfand and
  Ponomarev, with a reference to Manin.
  Statement. Classify ordered quadruples of subspaces of a finite-dimensional vector
  space up to a common linear isomorphism. The category admits direct-sum de-
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                    85

  composition into indecomposables and an explicit classification containing discrete
  families and regular one-parameter data. Over a general field, irreducible polyno-
  mial and rational-canonical data replace eigenvalue parameters; the classification
  is not a finite list of configurations.
  Reconstruction. Field-dependent regular parameters are retained, rather than as-
  suming an algebraically closed field without saying so.
  Fate. Gel’fand and Ponomarev (1971), received 21 October 1970, gives the match-
  ing canonical classification; it was already reported in earlier seminar record 1970-
  198. The 1986 passage is retrospective.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-696.4 The free modular lattice and its linear                                  Class E
representations
  Kind; source. definition; P001326
  The notes. Terms in e1 , . . . , er use sum and intersection, with equality by axioms; a
  representation preserves both operations.
  Statement. Let Dr be the free lattice in the variety of modular lattices on r gener-
  ators. A finite-dimensional linear representation over k is a lattice homomorphism
  ρ : Dr → L(V ), with L(V ) the subspace lattice. Thus a representation is exactly a
  configuration of r ordered subspaces of V , extended to all lattice terms. Abstract
  equality in Dr need not be the same relation as equality in every such representa-
  tion.
  Reconstruction. The field and finite-dimensional representation category are spec-
  ified.
  Fate. Gel’fand et al. (1987, Sec. 1, pp. 524–525) states these definitions. The
  project builds on Gel’fand and Ponomarev (1974).
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-696.5     Undecidable equality in free modular lattices                       Class E

  Kind; source. theorem; P001326
  The notes. The notes say the word-identity problem is undecidable for r ≥ 5.
  Statement. There is no algorithm deciding whether two lattice terms are equal in
  the free modular lattice on r generators when r ≥ 4. The result concerns equality
  modulo all modular identities, not equality in all finite-dimensional subspace lattices
  over a fixed field.
  Reconstruction. The distinct decision problems are separated; Ryzhikova's theorem
  is not a contradiction of the free-modular-lattice result.
  Fate. Herrmann (1983, Thm. 1, p. 513) proves the four-generator case and re-
  calls Freese's earlier r ≥ 5 theorem. The note is correct but gives a nonoptimal
  threshold already in 1986. Ryzhikova (1987, Theorem) establishes the contrasting
  decidability of subspace-lattice identities, submitted 16 May 1986 and published in
  1987.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: none; confidence: high
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                    86

1986-696.6 Finite equational axiomatization of linear                             Class E
equivalence
  Kind; source. problem; P001326
  The notes. Ryzhikova, Semenov's diploma student, is associated with the question
  whether linear equivalence can be formulated by finitely many axioms.
  Statement. In the equational interpretation, ask whether all identities valid in sub-
  space lattices of finite-dimensional rational vector spaces follow from finitely many
  lattice identities. Equivalently, is the lattice variety generated by these subspace
  lattices finitely based? The analogous class including all fields contains the rational
  case and is also generated by finite-dimensional lattices. The notebook does not fix
  a field or say whether axioms must be equations.
  Reconstruction. The equational and field quantifiers are explicit editorial specifica-
  tions. This is not the earlier decidability problem GP-1985-20.
  Fate. Herrmann (1984, Thm. 1.4), received February 1982 and revised August
  1983, proves that a modular variety containing all rational projective geometries
  cannot both be finitely based and be generated by its finite-dimensional members.
  This gives a negative answer to the stated standard interpretation. Ryzhikova
  (1987) answers a different question, algorithmic recognition, affirmatively.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 the finite equational basis question under
  the rational/all-field interpretation has a pre-1986 negative answer. Fixed positive-
  characteristic variants or unrestricted first-order axioms are not silently identified
  with this formulation.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: medium;
  problem GP-1986-35

1986-696.7 Morphisms and indecomposability of lattice                               Class E
representations
  Kind; source. definition; P001326
  The notes. A morphism V1 → V2 takes every represented subspace into its counter-
  part; indecomposability is introduced through direct sums.
  Statement. For representations ρi : Dr → L(Vi ), define Hom(ρ1 , ρ2 ) = {f ∈
  Homk (V1 , V2 ) : f (ρ1 (x)) ⊆ ρ2 (x) ∀x ∈ Dr }. It suffices to impose the inclusions on
  the generators. A direct-sum splitting must split all represented subspaces
  simultaneously; indecomposable means no nontrivial such splitting.
  Reconstruction. The requirement of simultaneous splitting prevents confusion with
  arbitrary decompositions of the ambient vector space.
  Fate. Gel’fand et al. (1987, Sec. 1, p. 525) gives exactly this category. It is the
  standard category underlying the earlier subspace-configuration work.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-696.12 Perfect elements are tested on indecomposable                         Class E
representations
  Kind; source. definition; P001327
  The notes. A perfect element is defined by ρ(a) = 0 or V for every representation,
  and compared to the center of a group ring.
  Statement. The standard definition requires ρ(a) ∈ {0, V } for every indecomposable
  finite-dimensional representation, over the fields under consideration. On a decom-
  posable representation different summands may take different alternatives, so the
  image can be a proper nonzero direct summand. The analogy with central idem-
  potents concerns such block selection; it is not an isomorphism of lattice and ring
  centers.
  Reconstruction. The omitted indecomposable restriction is a substantive correction
  confirmed against the original P001327 scan.
  Fate. Gel’fand and Ponomarev (1974) introduced these perfect elements. Gel’fand
  et al. (1987, Sec. 1, Prop. 3 and following discussion) restates the indecomposable
  qualifier and the construction from suitable upward-closed subsets of preprojec-
  tives.

1.31     A. V. Zelevinsky, 1986-11-24: The unitary dual of
         general linear groups
record 697; source pages P001327-P001331.
   The talk begins on P001328 and ends at the top of P001330, before Kazakov. It
reports the unitary-dual classification of Tadić and Vogan through determinant char-
acters, complementary series, and normalized parabolic induction, then discusses
square-integrable building blocks over local fields. Gelfand adds a kernel model for
invariant Hermitian forms. The main formulas are recoverable, but the absolute-value
normalization, modular character, center quotient, and segment exponents need cor-
rection or qualification.
1986-697.1     Unitary characters of the complex multiplicative                      Class E
group
  Kind; source. construction; P001328
  The notes. δ(z) = (z/|z|)k |z|iρ , k ∈ Z, ρ ∈ R, and u(δ, n) = δ ◦ det.
  Statement. Every continuous unitary character of C× has the displayed form,
  using the ordinary complex modulus. Composing with determinant gives a one-
  dimensional unitary representation of GLn (C). These are the u(δ, n) building blocks
  in the complex-field classification because square-integrable representations
  modulo center occur here only for GL1 (C).
  Reconstruction. Continuity and the ordinary-modulus convention are specified.
  Fate. Badulescu and Renard (2010, Secs. 1.2 and 9.1) explicitly gives these blocks
  and places them in the established Tadić–Vogan classification. The characters them-
  selves are classical.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.

1986-697.2 Stein complementary-series blocks and the complex                          Class E
absolute value
  Kind; source. construction; P001328
  The notes. The second family is induced from | det |α u(δ, n) ⊗ | det |−α u(δ, n), with 0 <
  α < 1/2.
  Statement. Let ν(g) = | det g|C = | det g|2 , where the rightmost modulus is ordinary.
  With normalized parabolic induction, π(u, α) = ν α u × ν −α u is irreducible and unita-

1986-697.3    Induction, modular normalization, and the relevant                 Class E
adjunction
  Kind; source. definition; P001328-P001329; I. M. Gelfand
  The notes. Gelfand defines induced functions by f (hg) = π(h)f (g), then says restric-
  tion and induction are dual functors.
  Statement. For parabolic P = M N in a reductive local group, normalized
                                                       1/2
  smooth induction uses an equivariance factor δP in addition to the inducing
  M -representation, with a consistent left/right convention. In the non-Archimedean
  smooth category its Frobenius adjunction involves the normalized Jacquet func-
  tor, not merely ordinary restriction to M . For finite groups ordinary induction
  and restriction satisfy the familiar Frobenius reciprocity. Function spaces and
  completions matter for locally compact groups.
  Reconstruction. The categorical and analytic qualifications distinguish an informal
  induction definition from the precise parabolic functor.
  Fate. Tadić (1986, Sec. 1) and Badulescu and Renard (2010, Secs. 2.4–3) fix the
  normalized parabolic conventions used in the classification. The notebook gives
  only the underlying equivariant-function picture.

1986-697.4 Irreducibility, exhaustiveness, and uniqueness of the                 Class E
complex unitary building blocks
  Kind; source. theorem; P001329
  The notes. Theorem (5) says all products of the two block types are irreducible and
  unitary, every irreducible unitary representation occurs, and equivalence means
  permutation of blocks.
  Statement. For GLn (C), take a finite multiset of determinant-character blocks u(δ, m)
  and complementary blocks π(u(δ, m), α), 0 < α < 1/2, whose degrees sum to n. Their
  normalized parabolic product is irreducible and unitarizable. Every irreducible uni-
  tary representation occurs, and the multiset of blocks is unique. This classifies irre-
  ducibles; general unitary representations require direct-integral multiplicity data.
  Reconstruction. Normalized induction, the local absolute value, and irreducibility
  rather than arbitrary unitary decompositions are specified.
  Fate. Vogan (1986) was published in October 1986. The Tadić approach circulated
  as MPI/SFB 85–22 in 1985; Badulescu and Renard (2010, Secs. 1.2, 7.2 and 12)
  gives the precise theorem and later proof history, including the separate ingredients
  needed for the Archimedean approach.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1986-697.5 Square integrability must be taken modulo the                           Class E
center
  Kind; source. definition; P001329
  The notes. Du is the set of square-integrable representations of GLk (F ), with square-
  integrable matrix coefficients.
  Statement. The relevant building blocks are irreducible unitary representations
  whose matrix coefficients are square-integrable on GLk (F )/Z, with unitary central
  character. They need not be square-integrable on the full group, whose center
  is noncompact. For F = C, only k = 1 occurs; for F = R, k = 1, 2 occur; non-
  Archimedean local fields have higher-degree discrete-series blocks.
  Reconstruction. The omitted center quotient is substantive and is restored explic-
  itly.
  Fate. Badulescu and Renard (2010, Secs. 7 and 9) uses square integrability modulo
  center throughout. Tadić (1986, Sec. 5) gives the non-Archimedean Langlands
  ingredients.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the

1986-697.6      Speh blocks as Langlands quotients and the correct                         Class E
segment
  Kind; source. construction; P001329
  The notes. u(δ, n) is the unique irreducible quotient of an induction with first expo-
  nent (n − 1)/2, second n/2 − 1, and final exponent −(n − 1)/2 without a final δ.
  Statement. For an irreducible unitary square-integrable-mod-center representation
  δ of GLk (F ), with F a local field, set u(δ, n) to be the unique Langlands quotient of
  ν (n−1)/2 δ × ν (n−3)/2 δ × · · · × ν −(n−1)/2 δ. Here ν = | det |F and the exponents decrease by
  one. The standard module is generally reducible; its quotient is the block used in
  the unitary classification.
  Reconstruction. The scan confirms the second exponent n/2 − 1, so replacing it by
  (n−3)/2 is a faithful-error correction, not a transcription claim; the last δ is restored.
  Fate. Badulescu and Renard (2010, Sec. 7.1, Eqs. (7.1)–(7.3)) gives the exact for-
  mula, with νδ replacing ν for general division-algebra inner forms. Tadić (1986, Sec.
  5) supplies the split non-Archimedean version.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
  hypotheses in the modern statement.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
  tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-697.8    Claims about other groups lack a fixed real form                    Class F

1986-697.11      Analytic continuation alone does not preserve                              Class E
positivity
 Kind; source. remark; P001330; I. M. Gelfand
 The notes. For ρ > 2 the notes demand analytic continuation and say the form will
 no longer be positive definite; a Fourier-side weighted norm is sketched.
 Statement. The homogeneous kernel admits meromorphic continuation as a tem-
 pered distribution, with poles at even positive values of ρ in real dimension two.
 Away from poles, its Fourier coefficient is proportional to 22−ρ πΓ(1 − ρ/2)/Γ(ρ/2); its
 sign depends on the parameter interval and any scalar normalization. Thus contin-
 uation is not automatically positive, but the blanket claim of nonpositivity for every
 ρ > 2 is also too strong. Invariant subquotients and moment-vanishing conditions
 require their own analysis.
 Reconstruction. The unnormalized continuation, its poles, and scalar sign are dis-
 tinguished from positivity of a specified representation-theoretic quotient.
 Fate. Gel’fand and Shilov (1964, Chs. II–III) provides the homogeneous-distribution
 framework; Knapp and Stein (1971) develops the intertwining and complementary-
 series context. The notebook gives no specific representation whose positivity
 range can be fully reconstructed.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026 this is an established result under the
 hypotheses in the modern statement.
 Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
 cation: publisher metadata checked; development: annotated; confidence: high

1.32     V. A. Kazakov, 1986-11-24: Unitary integrals and the
         Ising model on random planar maps
record 698; source pages P001329-P001334.
    The identifiable talk begins on P001330. It moves from a unitary integral with
several diagonal sources and reduced gauge models to the two-matrix representation
of an Ising model on fluctuating planar maps. The ordinary Harish-Chandra–Itzykson–
Zuber integral and biorthogonal-polynomial reduction are the calculational bridge.
P001329 belongs to the preceding discussion; the February lecture starts on P001333.
1986-698.1 The unitary integral with two independent diagonal                        Class C
sources

1986-698.2     Expansion near the identity and planar perturbation                  Class E
theory
  Kind; source. method; P001330
  The notes. The notes put Ω = eiA , isolate a Gaussian part, expand the rest of the
  exponential and the Haar measure, and retain planar diagrams.
  Statement. An expansion about a specified nondegenerate saddle of a unitary matrix
  integral produces Gaussian Wick contractions and interaction vertices, including
  the Jacobian of exponential coordinates. At fixed formal order, double-line contrac-
  tions are sorted by the Euler characteristic of their ribbon graph; planar terms are
  the leading powers of N under the usual matrix scaling.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                               95

 Reconstruction. The saddle and normalization conditions are necessary additions.
 The notebook does not justify a globally valid identity-centred expansion.
 Fate. This is the established matrix-model diagrammatic method of Bessis et al.
 (1980, Sections 2–3). It does not itself establish convergence or select the dominant
 saddle of the multi-source integral.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.

1986-698.3   One-site reduction of lattice gauge theory                        Class E

 Kind; source. claim; P001331
 The notes. A product of d unitary integrals with plaquette traces is asserted to be
 equivalent to the ordinary gauge theory as N → ∞.
 Statement. The Eguchi–Kawai loop-equation reduction requires, in addition to
 large-N factorization, the unbroken centre symmetry that annihilates open reduced
 loops. The unrestricted equivalence of the original one-site model at every coupling
 is false; in the relevant weak-coupling regime the centre symmetry can break.
 Fate. Eguchi and Kawai (1982) proposed the reduction in 1982. Bhanot et al. (1982,
 abstract and model construction) identified the symmetry-breaking obstruction and
 proposed quenching in the same year. This also connects with occurrences 1982-
 565.3–1982-565.6, but is not identical to the earlier specific free-energy problem.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
 Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
 cation: publisher metadata checked; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-698.4   Two-matrix Ising model on random quadrangulations                 Class A

 Kind; source. construction; P001331
 The notes. The integral has Gaussian coupling 2cuv and separate quartic vertices
 u4 + v 4 ; it is said to count two-coloured quadrangular decompositions with a weight
 for interfaces.
 Statement. Treated as a formal expansion of a normalized two-Hermitian-matrix
 Gaussian integral with positive-definite quadratic covariance, the connected free
 energy sums connected orientable ribbon graphs. Quartic vertices are assigned
 one of two colours; dualization gives quadrangulations with an Ising spin on each
 dual face or original vertex, according to convention. Graphs carry automorphism
 factors, and the mixed propagator fixes the domain-wall weight.
 Reconstruction. Formal expansion, connectedness, automorphism weights and the
 distinction between the Gaussian parameter c and an exponential Ising coupling
 have been supplied. The displayed e2cm cannot simply use the same c as the
 quadratic matrix coupling.

 The journal article appeared on 8 December 1986, shortly after the recorded 24

1986-698.8     Parametric planar free energy and its critical points                Class A

  Kind; source. claim; P001332
  The notes. The notes give g(z) = z/(1 − 3z)2 − c2 z + 3c2 z 3 , an integral expression for
  F , and locate singularities by g ′ (z) = 0.
  Statement. On the branch of the stated parametric solution analytic at the Gaussian
  point, critical points of the inverse relation z = z(g) occur where g ′ (z) = 0, unless a
  singularity or cancellation changes the local analysis. Extracting asymptotics of
  map coefficients additionally requires identifying the dominant singularity and the
  order of vanishing. The displayed free-energy integral depends on the partition-
  function normalization.
  Fate. The explicit two-matrix Ising solution is identified by Kazakov (1986). Its pub-
  lication metadata is verified, but a line-by-line match of the notebook free-energy
  normalization is not asserted. The inverse-function criterion explains the stated
  mechanism; the notebook alone does not justify the claimed global asymptotics.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the planar Ising matrix model is exactly
  solved. The present identification is of the model and critical-point mechanism;
  the precise additive and multiplicative normalization of the notebook expression
  remains an editorial qualification.

1986-700.2    Genus enumeration of oriented polygon gluings                         Class E

 Kind; source. question; P001333
 The notes. Pair the sides of an oriented 2n-gon with opposite orientation; cyclic
 rotations remain distinguishable. Count the resulting surfaces by genus.
 Statement. Let g (n) count perfect matchings of the labelled sides of a fixed oriented
 2n-gon, glued orientation-reversingly, whose quotient is a connected closed oriented
 surface of genus g. The number is zero for 2g > n. Labels fix a boundary root, so no
 quotient by rotation is taken.
 Fate. Harer and Zagier (1986, Theorem 2 and its recurrence) had already solved
 this enumeration in 1986. The later Kazakov lecture 1986-702 gives the matrix-
 integral derivation. The connection is to orbifold Euler characteristics of moduli
 spaces; Teichmüller space itself is contractible.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 exact; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high;

 Kind; source. method; P001336-P001337
 The notes. A determinant-n matrix sum is expanded in a Hecke eigenbasis; setting

 Statement. For even k > 2, the convergent holomorphic kernel obtained by summing

                                                        P
 and eigenvalues aj (n). Integrating the diagonal gives dim

 Fate. Zagier (1977, Section 2 and equations (14)–(19)) develops this kernel method.

1986-700.10    Binary quadratic forms in the trace calculation                       Class E

 Kind; source. construction; P001337-P001338
 The notes. Matrices of determinant n are reindexed by t = a + d and Q = [c, d − a, −b],
 with discriminant t2 − 4n, and the associated real-analytic kernel is integrated.
 Statement. The matrix-to-form substitution has disc(Q) = t2 − 4n. The elliptic con-
 tribution comes from negative discriminants with stabilizer weights. Positive non-
 square discriminants cancel in the specialized trace calculation, whereas positive
 squares contribute the divisor term; the scalar discriminant-zero contribution must
 also be retained. Sign pairs must be counted consistently with the initial factor 1/2.
 Fate. Zagier (1977, Section 1, equations (17)–(23), and Sections 3–4) explicitly sep-
 arates these cases. The notebook sentence about discriminants cannot be read as
 saying that all nonnegative contributions vanish.

1986-700.14 Completion, continuation, and poles of the                              Class E
Eisenstein series
  Kind; source. theorem; P001340-P001341
  The notes. The notes assert a functional equation using (2π)s Γ(s)E(z, s), and list poles
  at 1 and half of zeta zeros.
  Statement. The correct completion for the primitive sum is

                                E ∗ (z, s) = π −s Γ(s)ζ(2s)E(z, s)

  . It is invariant under s 7→ 1 − s, with its standard simple poles at 0 and 1. The
  uncompleted series has residue 3/π at 1; zeros of ζ(2s) give possible poles after
  division, subject to cancellation. Multiplying by Γ(s) alone does not give the stated
  symmetry.
  Fate. Zagier (1981, Section 1) supplies the exact completion and residue. The miss-
  ing zeta factor and the power of π are material errors in the recorded formula.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;

1986-700.15     Horocycle averages and the Riemann hypothesis                   Class C

  Kind; source. theorem; P001341-P001342
  The notes. The constant term is said to tend to the average over the fundamen-
  tal domain, with oscillations of size y 3/4 . The normalization 3/π disappears in one
  displayed formula.
  Statement.
       R       For suitable smooth compactly supported automorphic F , a0 (y) →
  (3/π) Γ\H F dµ as y ↓ 0. Mellin poles associated with zeta zeros control finer terms.
  A bound of the form OF (y 3/4−ε ) for all such tests is a Riemann-hypothesis strength
  assertion; it is not an unconditional rate for every measurable invariant function.
  Fate. Zagier (1981, Section 1 and the horocycle discussion) explains the connection
  between equidistribution remainders and zeta zeros. The current unresolved status
  of RH is independently recorded by Clay Mathematics Institute (2026).
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, qualitative equidistribution under the
  stated test-function hypotheses is established; the universal RH-strength remainder
  is conditional. The notebook should not be read as an announced proof of RH.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
  tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high; problem GP-1986-
  61
  Worth developing. The statement is useful as an exact conditional implication and

1986-700.16 Quadratic-field periods and toroidal automorphic                    Class E
forms
  Kind; source. construction; P001342
  The notes. Toroidal integrals of the Eisenstein series contain a quadratic-field zeta
  function, and therefore vanish at suitable zeros of ζ(s).
  Statement. For a nonsplit rational torus attached to a quadratic field, the appro-
  priately normalized toroidal period of an Eisenstein series factors into a Hecke
  L-function product. The toroidal subspace consists of automorphic forms whose
  periods over every such torus vanish. Analytic continuation and the exact normal-
  ization are required at zeros and poles; one cannot simply substitute a singular
  quotient.
  Fate. Zagier (1981, Sections 2–4) develops the proposal already before the talk.
  Cornelissen and Lorscheid (2011, main theorem), first circulated in June 2009 and
  revised in August 2011, identifies the Eisenstein-derivative part through zero mul-
  tiplicities and the cuspidal part through central L-values.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the later structural theorem clarifies the
  toroidal space, including derivatives at multiple zeros. It does not prove that all
  zeta zeros lie on the critical line.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  exact; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-700.17     The toroidal spectral strategy for the Riemann                  Class C
hypothesis
  Kind; source. question; P001342-P001343
  The notes. The notes form a span W of quadratic-form transforms and an orthog-
  onal complement V , note Laplacian invariance, and suggest that unitarity of the
  corresponding representation would imply RH.
  Statement. The recognizable strategy seeks a spectral realization of the toroidal
  automorphic space that forces the relevant Eisenstein parameters onto the critical
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                  104

  line. The necessary conclusion is temperedness of the relevant constituents; uni-
  tarity alone allows complementary-series representations and is insufficient. One
  must specify the topology, invariant pairing, spectral identification and exclusion
  of non-tempered constituents. Formal invariance under the Laplacian proves none
  of these requirements.
  Fate. Zagier (1981, final sections) gives the earlier strategy and its difficulties. Cor-
  nelissen and Lorscheid (2011, Introduction and main results) identifies the toroidal
  spectrum and explicitly states the RH connection in terms of temperedness. It does
  not prove that needed restriction. Clay Mathematics Institute (2026) confirms that
  RH remains open.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the algebraic toroidal spectrum is substan-
  tially understood, but the required critical-line spectral restriction is unproved. The
  notebook’s unqualified appeal to unitarity does not establish RH.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; veri-
  fication: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: medium; problem
  GP-1986-61
  Worth developing. Worth preserving as a precisely conditional spectral strategy;
  the notebook leaves the functional-analytic object too unspecified for a new proof
  attempt here.

record 701; source pages P001342-P001350.
   The lecture connects even zeta-values, Eisenstein series and Mellin transforms

begins on P001349.

1986-701.2 Even and negative zeta-values and moduli Euler                                Class E
characteristics
  Kind; source. theorem; P001343
  The notes. Bernoulli formulas for ζ(2n) and ζ(1 − 2g) are related to the Euler charac-
  teristic of the moduli space; the list gives ζ(−3) = 1/252.
  Statement. For n ≥ 1, ζ(2n) = (−1)n+1 B2n (2π)2n /[2(2n)!] and ζ(1 − 2n) = −B2n /(2n). For
  g ≥ 2, χorb (Mg,1 ) = ζ(1 − 2g) and χorb (Mg ) = ζ(1 − 2g)/(2 − 2g). Thus ζ(−3) = 1/120, while
  ζ(−5) = −1/252.
  Fate. The moduli-space formula is the main theorem of Harer and Zagier (1986),
  published before this lecture. Marking one point and using the orbifold Euler char-

1986-701.3 Eisenstein products and recurrences for even                        Class E
zeta-values
  Kind; source. method; P001343-P001344
  The notes. Identities such as E42 = E8 and E4 E6 = E10 yield recurrences for even
  zeta-values.
  Statement. Level-one dimensions give E42 = E8 and E4 E6 = E10 for normalized Eisen-
  stein series. Comparing their Fourier coefficients and the Bernoulli normalization
  produces convolution identities for divisor sums and even zeta-values. At weights
  with cusp forms, the Eisenstein product generally has a nonzero cuspidal compo-
  nent, which must be retained.

  framework used later in the lecture. The assertion is an established technique,

1986-701.7    Rankin unfolding of products of Eisenstein series                    Class E

 Kind; source. method; P001345-P001347
 The notes. The Petersson product of f with G2n Gk−2n is unfolded to a Dirichlet series
 involving am σk−1−2n (m). The written range makes one weight negative.
 Statement. For even k and integers n with 2n ≥ 4, k − 2n ≥ 4, pair the holomorphic
 Eisenstein product with a weight-k cusp form. Absolute convergence in an auxiliary
 parameter, followed where necessary by analytic continuation, justifies unfolding.
 Fourier integration yields a Gamma factor Γ(k − 1) = (k − 2)!, with conjugations
 consistent with the Petersson convention.
 Fate. Kohnen and Zagier (1984, Rankin method and products of Eisenstein series)
 gives this argument. The notebook’s inequality k/2 < n ≤ k − 2 is incompatible with
 its chosen weights; an admissible basic range is 2 ≤ n ≤ k/2 − 2.

  The notes. The divisor-sum Dirichlet series factors into two L-values divided by an

1986-701.9     Three rational structures on the cusp-form space                Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001347-P001348
  The notes. The lecture distinguishes rational Fourier coefficients and rational even
  and odd periods, and cites Kohnen–Zagier under the title “Modular forms with ra-
  tional coefficients.
  Statement. The Fourier expansion defines a rational structure on level-one cusp
  forms. Period polynomials, decomposed by parity, give two further rational struc-
  tures after choosing the appropriate period scalings; Hecke equivariance relates
  them. The critical-period length for a weight-w form is w − 2, so writing S2k while
  keeping the range 0, . . . , k − 2 mixes conventions.
  Fate. The explicitly cited work is Kohnen and Zagier (1984), whose actual title is
  Modular forms with rational periods, published in 1984, not a new 1987 result. It
  contains the rational-structure comparison rather than the claimed equality of all
  normalized critical values.

 The notes. The lecture returns to pairings of 2n polygon sides and counts them by

1986-702.2    Gaussian integration by parts and Wick contractions                  Class E
 Kind; source. method; P001350-P001351
 The notes. A Gaussian Hermitian matrix integral and an integration-by-parts iden-
 tity produce pairings of the factors in a trace.
 Statement. For a centred Hermitian Gaussian matrix with covariance EMij Mkl =
 N −1 δil δjk , Wick’s rule pairs entries and each contraction contributes that covariance.
 Equivalently, Gaussian integration by parts differentiates a polynomial observable
 against its covariance. The measure is normalized to expectation one; treating all
 complex off-diagonal entries as independent real entries would give wrong con-
 stants.
 Fate. Harer and Zagier (1986, matrix-integral proof of Theorem 2) and Bessis et al.
 (1980, Gaussian diagrammatics) give this exact combinatorial method, established
 before the talk.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 exact; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

  Kind; source. remark; P001352
  The notes. A remark attributes a Markov-process approach to Parisi.
  Statement. The identifiable method realizes a Euclidean field or matrix distribu-

1986-703.3      Trivial zeros and Bernoulli special values                               Class E

  The notes. The functional equation gives simple zeros at negative even integers,

1986-703.5 Borel regulators and derivatives at negative even                       Class E
integers
  Kind; source. theorem; P001356
  The notes. The corrected line relates ζ ′ (−2n) to the covolume of the image of K4n+1 (Z)
  in R.
  Statement. For n ≥ 1, the torsion-free part of K4n+1 (Z) has rank one. Under the
  Borel regulator with the corresponding Tate normalization, its image is a lattice in
  R, and the nonzero leading zeta-value ζ ′ (−2n) agrees with its regulator covolume
  up to a nonzero rational factor and the declared orientation convention. Torsion is
  killed by the real regulator.
  Fate. Borel (1977, Section 6, especially 6.2–6.4) proves the commensurability state-
  ment; the paper was received in November 1976 and published in 1977. The note-
  book’s replacement of ζ(1 − 2n) by a derivative at −2n is mathematically meaningful
  and should be preserved.

  The notes. The displayed identity makes (1 − pn−1 )ζ(1 − n) a moment of one Zp -valued

1986-703.7     Components of p-adic weight space                                   Class E

  Kind; source. definition; P001356
  The notes. The units are split into (1 + pZp ) and the (p − 1)-st roots of unity, giving
  p − 1 components of the character space.

  Fate. Gelbart et al. (2012, discussion of weight space and the Iwasawa algebra)

  prime restriction is missing from the notes.

1986-703.9 Finite-image Artin representations and ramified                           Class E
Euler factors
  Kind; source. definition; P001357
  The notes. A continuous complex representation of the absolute Galois group is lo-
  calized at primes; inertia and Frobenius are introduced, followed by the unramified
  determinant factor.
  Statement. A continuous finite-dimensional representation ρ : GQ → GL(V ) with
  the usual complex topology has finite image. It is unramified outside finitely many
  primes. At every prime, the Artin factor is Lp (ρ, s) = det(1 − p−s ρ(Frp )|V Ip )−1 , with
  a consistent arithmetic-Frobenius convention. The choice of decomposition group
  changes the data by conjugacy. The notebook formula on all of V applies at unram-
  ified primes.
  Fate. Gun et al. (2025, Introduction) gives the full inertia-invariant definition and
  finite-Galois setup. The formulation is classical; the 2025 paper is used to verify
  the exact general convention and current status, not as the origin of Artin theory.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1986-703.10      Dirichlet and Dedekind examples of Artin formalism                  Class E

  Kind; source. theorem; P001357-P001358
  The notes. One-dimensional characters give Dirichlet functions; inducing the trivial
  character from GK gives ζK ; direct sums multiply L-functions.
  Statement. For a finite-order one-dimensional character of GQ , the Artin L-function
  is the primitive Dirichlet L-function associated with its conductor. For any number
                 GQ
  field K, L(IndGK 1, s) = ζK (s). Moreover L(ρ1 ⊕ ρ2 , s) = L(ρ1 , s)L(ρ2 , s). Using an im-
  primitive character modulo a larger integer removes extra Euler factors and must
  be distinguished from the primitive Artin function.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                               116

  Fate. Kudla (2003, Section 1) explains primitive and imprimitive characters, while
  Mantilla-Soler (2019, Section 2) gives the induced Galois representation for the
  Dedekind zeta function.

1986-703.11 An Artin L-function determines the representation                   Class E
but not the field
  Kind; source. theorem; P001358
  The notes. The notes explicitly distinguish recovering ρ from L(ρ, s) and the failure
  to recover K from ζK (s).
  Statement. Finite-image complex representations of GQ with identical Artin L-
  functions are isomorphic: equality of Dirichlet coefficients determines unramified
  Frobenius traces, Chebotarev determines the finite-group character, and complex
  finite-group representations are semisimple. However nonconjugate subgroups
  can induce isomorphic permutation representations, yielding nonisomorphic
  arithmetically equivalent number fields.
  Fate. Mantilla-Soler (2019, Theorem 2.5, Corollary 2.8 and Section 3 examples)
  gives this argument and explicit field phenomena, tracing the arithmetic-
  equivalence criterion to Perlis (1977) and Gassmann. The later paper confirms
  rather than originates the notebook’s distinction.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-703.12     Artin holomorphy                                                Class C

  Kind; source. conjecture; P001358
  The notes. All complex Artin functions are said to be meromorphic; for nontrivial
  irreducible ρ, holomorphy everywhere is conjectured.
  Statement. Brauer induction gives meromorphic continuation to C of every finite-
  image complex Artin L-function. Artin’s conjecture asserts that L(ρ, s) is entire
  when ρ is nontrivial and irreducible. Trivial summands must be excluded because
  they contribute a Dedekind-zeta pole. Monomial representations and many further
  classes are known, but the general conjecture remains open.
  Fate. Gun et al. (2025, Introduction and Theorems in Section 1), published 17 Febru-
  ary 2025, explicitly distinguishes the classical meromorphic-continuation theorem
  from the still-open holomorphy assertion and gives new partial criteria.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, meromorphic continuation is established
  for all complex Artin representations, while the general nontrivial irreducible holo-
  morphy conjecture is unresolved.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: annotated; confidence: high; problem GP-1986-64
  Worth developing. The exact finite-image formulation and the boundary between
  Brauer meromorphy and entire continuation deserve preservation; no general proof
  is attempted here.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                       117

1986-703.13      The scope of the l-adic continuation question                          Class F

  Kind; source. remark; P001358
  The notes. For l-adic representations, the notes say that even meromorphic contin-
  uation is unknown.
  Statement. This sentence cannot apply uniformly to arbitrary continuous `-adic rep-
  resentations without additional conditions. To formulate the intended arithmetic
  problem one needs, at a minimum, a representation unramified outside finitely
  many places, suitable algebraic Frobenius eigenvalues and a complex realization;
  the geometric or motivic setting supplies the usual conjectural analytic continuation
  problem. These hypotheses are not stated in the notebook.
  Fate. The adjoining Artin discussion, verified in Gun et al. (2025), concerns finite-
  image complex representations and cannot settle this broader question. No specific
  motivic representation or exact conjecture is identified here.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the unqualified sentence is not a well-
  defined universal theorem or conjecture. Important geometric cases are known,
  but none is specified sufficiently here for a precise fate assignment.
  Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: thematic only;
  verification: unresolved lead; development: none; confidence: medium

1986-703.14      The completed Artin functional equation                                Class E

  Kind; source. theorem; P001358
  The notes. The last formula relates L∗ (ρ, s) to L∗ (ρ∗ , 1 − s), with an epsilon factor
  containing Gauss sums.
  Statement. For a finite-image complex Artin representation over Q, let a± be the
  dimensions of the ±1-eigenspaces of complex conjugation and A(ρ) its Artin con-
  ductor. With ΓR (s) = π −s/2 Γ(s/2), set Λ(ρ, s) = A(ρ)s/2 ΓR (s)a+ ΓR (s + 1)a− L(ρ, s). Then
  Λ(ρ, s) = W (ρ)Λ(ρ∗ , 1 − s) meromorphically, where |W (ρ)| = 1. If the conductor is
  omitted from the completion, the epsilon factor depends on s.
  Fate. The Artin analytic framework in Gun et al. (2025) distinguishes known func-
  tional equations from conjectural holomorphy; the local-global normalization is il-
  lustrated in Kudla (2003, Sections 3–4). The notebook has not fixed which conductor
  factor is absorbed in L∗ .
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.

1986-705.2    Marchenko dressing and its solvability conditions                   Class E

 Kind; source. construction; P001359-P001360
                                                          R
 The notes. The integral equation is K(x, z) + F (x + z) + K(x, s)F (s + z) ds = 0, with
 three proposed integration conventions, Ft = 8Fξξξ , and u = 2(d/dx)K(x, x).
 Statement. In the right Marchenko convention the integral is over [x, ∞). Assume
 sufficient smoothness and decay to differentiate and integrate by parts, and invert-
 ibility of the corresponding integral operator so that the normalized equation has
 a unique solution. The diagonal formula then produces the potential for the con-
 sistently signed Lax system. The left and half-difference prescriptions require their
 own boundary or formal-integral conventions; they are not unconditional solutions
 for every function F .
 Fate. Zakharov and Shabat (1974, Section 1, equations (4)–(6) and Theorems 1–2)
 develops the general dressing scheme cited explicitly in the lecture. The notebook
 omits the unique-solvability and boundary assumptions and carries over the KdV
 time-sign inconsistency. Its extension to growing exponentials is a formal prescrip-
 tion, not a convergent integral on an infinite interval.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 partial; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-705.4    The normalized nonlocal d-bar problem                               Class E

 Kind; source. method; P001360-P001361
                                                                  R
 The notes. The Fourier-transformed kernel leads to ∂¯λ χ = χ(η)T (η, λ) dη dη̄, with
 χ(∞) = 1; the coefficient of λ−1 determines u.
 Statement. Choose an area-form convention and a kernel for which the normalized
           ¯
 nonlocal ∂-problem    is uniquely solvable, for example in a Fredholm setting with no
 normalized homogeneous solution. Its Cauchy transform must use the matching
                                ¯
 distributional normalization ∂(1/z)   = πδ0 . Differentiating a uniquely solvable prob-
 lem with compatible parameter dependence supplies the auxiliary linear equations.
 The potential is extracted from the first coefficient at infinity after fixing the oscil-

1986-705.5 Commuting rational flows and Riemann-surface                           Class E

 Kind; source. construction; P001361-P001362
 The notes. The kernel evolves by Txi = Ii (η)T − T Ii (λ), with mutually commuting
 rational matrix functions; the notes also allow functions on a Riemann surface.

1986-705.6     First-order differentiation in a variable spectral                          Class E
parameter
 Kind; source. method; P001362
 The notes. The new proposal is Li = ∂xi + Ki (λ) + fi (λ)∂λ , with rational coefficients
 and moving poles; the gravitational example is attributed to Belinsky and Zakharov.
 Statement. The compatibility equation for such operators contains both matrix cur-
 vature and the commutator of the scalar spectral vector fields. Once an ansatz for
 poles and residues is fixed, its coefficients give nonlinear equations in the defor-
 mation variables. The count of equations and unknowns and solvability depend on
 that ansatz, so they are not universal consequences of writing [Li , Lj ] = 0.
 Fate. Burtsev et al. (1987, Introduction, equations (1.9)–(1.10), and subsequent con-
 structions) treats inverse scattering with a variable spectral parameter and includes
 the gravitational motivation. The paper was received on 6 February 1986 and pub-
 lished in March 1987, so this first-order part was circulating before the lecture,
 even though the notebook labels the wider proposal new.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 partial; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-705.7     The zero-curvature condition for spectral differential                      Class E
operators
 Kind; source. claim; P001362
 The notes. For Li = ∂xi +Mi (λ, ∂λ ), the notes write [Li , Lj ] = 0 if and only if [Mi , Mj ] = 0.
 Statement. The full condition is ∂xi Mj − ∂xj Mi + [Mi , Mj ] = 0. It reduces to the writ-
 ten commutativity condition only when the Mi are independent of all deformation
 variables, or when their cross derivatives cancel for a specified reason. Rationality
 in λ alone does not remove these terms.
 Fate. This is the elementary compatibility identity used in the variable-spectral-
 parameter framework of Burtsev et al. (1987). The sentence becomes correct for
 the constant-coefficient-in-x example that follows, but not for the preceding claim
 about freely moving poles.

1986-705.10    Degenerating soliton kernels to rational solutions                Class F

 Kind; source. method; P001363
 The notes. The prescription begins with Gelfand–Levitan–Marchenko equations in
 the spectral variable, takes n-soliton solutions, and degenerates them to rational
 solutions; the next step is to construct the kernels K(λ, λ′ , xi ) and F (λ, λ′ , xi ).
 Statement. A definite degeneration theorem must specify the nonlinear system, a
 family of scattering or kernel data, the rescaling and coalescence parameters, and
 the topology in which the potentials or wave functions converge. None of these
 limiting data is fixed in this passage. The assertion is therefore a proposed method,
 rather than a stated theorem for all rational solutions.
 Fate. Zakharov and Shabat (1974, Section 3) gives explicit finite-rank soliton ker-
 nels in the established dressing method. That construction is an antecedent, not
 an exact publication match for the unspecified spectral-variable rational limit sug-
 gested here.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, rational limits of soliton constructions are
 a recognizable topic, but this notebook passage does not select one equation or
 degeneration theorem whose precise fate can be assigned.

1986-706.1   Matrix expectation and the integration contour                    Class E

 Kind; source. definition; P001363
 The notes. The matrix integral has weight exp[−(N /λ) Tr V (A)], while A∗ = −A; the
 loop observable is ψ(t) = N −1 Tr etA .
 Statement. A rigorous one-matrix model can be defined on Hermitian matrices with
 real confining polynomial V and λ > 0, normalized by its finite partition function. A
 skew-Hermitian convention instead needs a compatible contour and potential ob-
 tained by A = iH; it is not the same real-eigenvalue integral without changing these
 data. Formal power-series expectations provide another explicitly distinct interpre-
 tation.
 Reconstruction. The confining Hermitian model is the precise setting used for the
 analytical statements below; it is an editorial choice, not a silent change to the
 recorded anti-Hermitian line.
 Fate. Brézin et al. (1978, Sections 2–3) defines the Hermitian planar model and its
 eigenvalue reduction. The notebook switches between anti-Hermitian gauge fields
 and real-eigenvalue matrix formulas without declaring the contour change.

 Kind; source. problem; P001364; I. M. Gelfand, attributed to T. Alekseevskaya

  Kind; source. theorem; P001365
  The notes. Normalized trace correlations are replaced by the product of their ex-

1986-706.8 Wilson loops as gauge-invariant parallel transport                   Class E

  Kind; source. definition; P001366
                                                                          H
  The notes. The matrix exponential is replaced by W (c) = N −1 Tr P exp c Aµ dxµ .
  Statement. For a smooth connection in a unitary representation and a piecewise
  smooth closed path, path ordering defines its parallel transport. Its normalized

1986-706.9     The Makeenko–Migdal loop equation and its scope                 Class E
  Kind; source. method; P001366-P001367
  The notes. The lecture proposes repeating the matrix integration-by-parts argu-
  ment for the gauge-field functional integral and ends with an abbreviated functional
  derivative.
  Statement. The formal gauge-field Schwinger–Dyson identity relates an area deriva-
  tive of a Wilson loop to a contact term splitting the contour at coincident points.
  In the planar limit, factorization replaces the two-loop expectation by a product. A
  precise continuum equation must specify regularization, renormalization and treat-
  ment of cusps and intersections; deriving this identity is not a solution of four-
  dimensional Yang–Mills theory.
  Fate. The original equation was published in Makeenko and Migdal (1979); Ma-
  keenko (2008, Section 3.1, equation (3.2), and Section 3.3) gives the regularized
  interpretation and its limitations. The lecture is an exposition of the established
  loop-equation method.

  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.

1986-708.1    Finite-range and mean-field spin-glass Hamiltonians                 Class E

  Kind; source. definition; P001368
  The notes. The lecture starts with nearest-neighbour random couplings and then
  passes to all-pairs Gaussian couplings scaled by N −1/2 .
  Statement. The finite-range Edwards–Anderson model uses a specified lattice and
  neighbouring pairs. The zero-field Sherrington–KirkpatrickP   model has σ ∈ {−1, 1}N ,
  independent standard Gaussian Jij , and HN (σ) = N −1/2 i<j Jij σi σj . Independence,
  centering and the summation convention determine the normalization. The ther-
  modynamic ground energy is normalized by N .
  Fate. The precise Gaussian mean-field setting is the one treated in Auffinger and
  Chen (2017, Introduction) and Talagrand (2006). Their theorems do not automati-
  cally solve the short-range model introduced in the first line.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.

1986-708.3 Exact finite-volume ground-state degeneracy and                        Class E
near-ground states
  Kind; source. claim; P001368
  The notes. The notes describe many ground states with energies differing by about
  1/N , and assert positive limiting entropy of their set.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                 128

  Statement. For finite N ≥ 2, independent continuous Gaussian couplings in the
  zero-field complete-graph SK model give almost surely exactly one minimizing pair
  {σ, −σ}. Consequently the entropy per spin of exact minimizers is N −1 log 2 → 0.
  States with slightly different energies are near-ground states; their count depends
  on a specified energy window and normalization, which the notebook does not pro-
  vide.
  Fate. [proved here] For τ 6= ±σ, some product τi τj differs from σi σj , so the energy
  difference is a nondegenerate Gaussian and vanishes with probability zero. There
  are finitely many pairs; spin-flip symmetry supplies the only forced equality. Inde-
  pendently, equality of every pair product forces τi /σi to be constant in i, checking
  the algebraic step. This elementary annotation corrects the literal sentence without
  replacing the much richer asymptotic-state problem treated in Panchenko (2013).
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the finite-volume claim is false under the
  stated Gaussian SK assumptions. The notebook’s unspecified near-ground energy
  window is insufficient to define a separate quantitative entropy problem.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
  cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-708.4    Ultrametricity of the limiting overlap array                       Class B

  Kind; source. conjecture; P001368
  The notes. For three states, two overlaps are equal and the third is larger; the
  statement is attributed to Parisi and Virasoro and phrased in terms of ground states.
  Statement. Let a random probability measure on the unit ball of a separable Hilbert
  space satisfy the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities for all bounded overlap tests. For
  independent replicas with overlaps Rab , R12 ≥ min(R13 , R23 ) almost surely. Permuting
  the replicas gives the ultrametric triple property. The hypothesis is a statement
  about the thermodynamic Gibbs or asymptotic overlap measure, not about every
  finite set of near-minimizers.
  Fate. Panchenko (2013, Theorem 1), first circulated in December 2011 and pub-
  lished in 2013, proves this implication without a finite-support assumption on the
  overlap distribution. Its introduction explains the averaging or small-perturbation
  mechanisms producing the identities in spin-glass models. These precise hypothe-
  ses prevent an unjustified universal finite-ground-state claim.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the ultrametricity theorem under the
  Ghirlanda–Guerra identities is established. The notebook’s informal state termi-
  nology is interpreted through this rigorous limiting framework, not as an exact
  statement for all finite samples.

1986-707.3   The local dimension of supermoduli                                 Class E

 Kind; source. theorem; P001369
 The notes. The corrected dimension in the notebook is (3g − 3|2g − 2).
 Statement. For a smooth unpunctured super Riemann surface of genus g ≥ 2, the
 supermoduli stack has local dimension 3g − 3|2g − 2. Its reduced data include a
 spin structure, and finite automorphisms require a stack or orbifold interpretation.
 Genus 0 and 1 require automorphism terms; punctures alter the dimension.
 Fate. Witten (2012, Section 2.2, equations (2.24)–(2.27)) derives the count from
 deformation cohomology. Crane and Rabin (1988, Introduction and Section 3) dis-
 tinguishes the already known local index count from its global super-Teichmüller
 construction, published in 1988. The bare dimension assertion is therefore E.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.

1986-707.5   Riemann–Roch and nonfree cohomology modules                       Class A

 Kind; source. theorem; P001369-P001370
 The notes. Gelfand asks for the function ring on a compact supercurve. Schwarz
 warns that kernels of equations over a Grassmann algebra need not be free, and
 only then discusses Riemann–Roch dimensions.
 Statement. For a family over an odd base, cohomology is a module over the base
 algebra and need not be free. Ranks can be used only on loci where the relevant
 direct images are locally free and commute with base change. The correct general
 invariant is the cohomological index or determinant/Berezinian of cohomology, with
 parity retained; a numerical dimension formula does not automatically describe the
 full function algebra.
 Fate. The warning in the notebook is substantive and should be preserved. The
 general supercurve theory is identified by Rosly et al. (1988), published in March
 1988; its original full text was not recovered, so an exact theorem-number match is
 not claimed. Witten (2012, Section 3.4) verifies the cohomological formulation and
 its qualifications.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
 Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
 partial; verification: publisher metadata checked; development: annotated; confi-
 dence: medium

1986-707.6   Super Abelian differentials, primitives, and periods              Class A

 Kind; source. construction; P001370
 The notes. Sections transforming like DΦ are called Abelian differentials. Primitives
 on the universal cover give periods, followed by Jacobian and Picard constructions.
 Statement. The superconformal distribution determines the appropriate line bundle
 of differentials and its Berezinian interpretation. Periods are defined from compat-
 ible closed differential representatives and cycles of the reduced surface. A nor-
 malized super period matrix has additional spin and nonsingularity conditions and
 can develop poles when the relevant Dirac operator acquires zero modes; it is not
 an everywhere regular coordinate on all supermoduli.
 Fate. Rosly et al. (1988) is the near-contemporary publication match for this the-
 ory. Witten (2012, Section 8, especially the end of 8.3) supplies a readable exact
 account of periods and the pole qualification. The 1988 paper is verified at meta-
 data/abstract level; the later technical account was opened and read.
 Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
 the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.

                                        e, eb} = τ eb + τ eb is said to admit local coordinates

1.43     D. V. Alekseevskii, 1987-04-13: Quaternionic
         structures and integrability
record 709; source pages P001370-P001375.
    The Alekseevskii heading on P001371 introduces the failure of a naive quaternionic
differentiability condition, quaternionic projective changes of coordinates, and the in-
variant rank-three bundle of almost complex structures. The discussion distinguishes
torsion-free quaternionic geometry from a chosen hypercomplex triple and includes a
twistor-style integrability proposal.
1986-709.1     The rigidity of naive quaternionic differentiability              Class E

  Kind; source. claim; P001371
  The notes. Transition maps have differentials that are morphisms of left quater-
  nionic modules. The notes conclude that all automorphisms are linear and that
  simple connectedness gives a linear space.
  Statement. For a connected open set, sufficiently smooth maps with everywhere
  quaternionic-linear differential are affine in fixed quaternionic coordinates. Trans-
  lations must be allowed. An affine atlas on a simply connected manifold supplies
  a developing map into Hn , not automatically a global identification with all of Hn ;
  completeness is an additional global requirement.
  Fate. Sudbery (1979, Section 3, Theorem 1) gives the precise one-variable affine-
  rigidity result. Salamon (1986, Introduction and Section 1) explains why such affine
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                 134

  structures are too restrictive for quaternionic geometry. The notebook’s local point
  is right, but “linear omits translations and the global simply-connected conclusion
  omits completeness.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
  cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-709.2    Quaternionic projective charts and inversion                        Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001371-P001372
  The notes. The space of quaternionic lines is labelled CP n ; the two charts on its
  projective line are related by q 7→ q −1 , with derivative −q −1 (dq)q −1 .
  Statement. The space is HP n . On HP 1 , inversion is a transition map and its differen-
  tial has multiplication on both sides. It generally fails to be a morphism of one fixed
  left H-module structure. Its differential does preserve the appropriate quaternionic
  structure up to rotation, which motivates the enlarged structure group.
  Fate. Salamon (1986, Section 1, projective-coordinate construction) gives exactly
  this motivation and the group GL(n, H)Sp(1). The manuscript’s use of C is a faithful
  notation error, not a translation issue.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.

1986-709.3    The ambiguous sphere obstruction                                    Class F

  Kind; source. remark; P001372
  The notes. The topological explanation says that S 3 would have three complex struc-

  Statement. An almost complex structure requires even real tangent dimension, so
  the stated S 3 cannot carry even one. The surrounding projective-line discussion

  Fate. The projective geometry in Salamon (1986) supplies context but does not

1986-709.4 Almost quaternionic structures without a                              Class E
distinguished triple
  Kind; source. definition; P001372
  The notes. A quaternionic subalgebra of End(V ) is specified, but its isomorphism
  with H and the individual I, J, K are not fixed.
  Statement. On a real 4n-manifold, an almost quaternionic structure is equivalently
  a rank-three subbundle Q ⊂ End(T M ) locally spanned by I, J, K satisfying I 2 = J 2 =
  K 2 = −1, IJ = K = −JI, together with the associated rank-four algebra R Id ⊕Q.
  Changing an oriented orthonormal local triple rotates it by SO(3). A global chosen
  triple is extra hypercomplex-type data.
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                  135

  Fate. Salamon (1986, Section 1) formulates the same invariant reduction and dis-
  tinguishes it from an integrable GL(n, H)-structure. The notebook’s explicit warning
  not to choose I, J, K globally is essential.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
  the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-709.5     The general and metric quaternionic structure                       Class E
groups
  Kind; source. theorem; P001372
  The notes. The notes display both GL(1, H)GL(n, H) and Sp(1)Sp(n), while attaching
  the latter to the earlier module-linearity definition.
  Statement. The stabilizer of the quaternionic subalgebra is GL(n, H)Sp(1), with
  the common central {±1} identified; positive scalar factors can be absorbed into
  GL(n, H). After a compatible positive definite metric is chosen, the group reduces
  to Sp(n)Sp(1). Preserving a fixed left module structure alone gives GL(n, H), not the
  compact metric group.
  Fate. Salamon (1986, Section 1) explicitly identifies these groups and their rela-
  tion. The compact-group line in the notebook has a missing metric condition and
  an incorrect reference to definition (1).
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
  Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
  tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-709.6     The special role of four dimensions                                 Class E

  Kind; source. construction; P001372
  The notes. Every four-dimensional Riemannian manifold is said to have two quater-
  nionic structures because SO(4) is a product.
  Statement. For an oriented Riemannian four-manifold, the self-dual and anti-self-
  dual rank-three bundles of two-forms give the two pointwise quaternionic families.
  The group relation is SO(4) ∼ = (Sp(1) × Sp(1))/{(1, 1), (−1, −1)}. Reversing orientation
  exchanges the choices. A quaternionic structure in dimension four encodes an ori-
  ented conformal class, not a unique metric.
  Fate. Salamon (1986, Section 1 and the four-dimensional discussion) gives the ex-
  ceptional relation Sp(1)Sp(1) = SO(4). The quotient and orientation data must ac-
  company the notebook’s shorthand “product.
  Present status. As of 12 November 2026, the precise statement above is established;
  the qualifications distinguish it from the abbreviated notebook formulation.
  Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
  partial; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1986-709.7 Torsion-free quaternionic connections and twistor                       Class E
integrability
  Kind; source. definition; P001373
  The notes. The condition for 1-integrability is a torsion-free connection preserving
  the quaternionic subalgebra; parallel transport preserves it, and the connection
  need not be metric.
  Statement. For n > 1, a quaternionic manifold is an almost quaternionic 4n-manifold
  admitting a torsion-free connection preserving Q. This is weaker than a flat projec-
  tive quaternionic atlas and does not choose a metric. Its associated sphere bundle
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1986–87                                                 136

  of complex structures has an integrable twistor complex structure. In dimension
  four the analogous twistor integrability additionally requires the appropriate self-
  duality condition on the conformal curvature.
  Fate. Salamon (1986, Sections 2–3 and Corollary 7.4) establishes the tor-
  sion/curvature framework and the twistor construction, published before the
  lecture in 1986. The notebook’s explicit “not necessarily metric is correct and
  prevents a conflation with quaternionic K”ahler geometry.

1986-709.8     Goncharov on complexified quaternionic cones                       Class F

  Kind; source. remark; P001373; A. B. Goncharov
  The notes. For real-analytic M , Goncharov forms a cone from vectors v + iJv in a
